## Founder Ad = Founder's Argument, Not Raw Footage
Use when: Planning a founder-led ad concept for a Meta acquisition campaign and deciding what the ad actually needs to communicate.
- Treat the founder ad as a constructed argument rather than documentary footage of the founder. The source defines it as a structured presentation of the founder's case, not just a camera pointed at them.
- Build the argument from three elements: the origin (why the business started), the unique mechanism (why the solution works, explained by the founder or an expert), and an adversarial angle (tearing down competitors or industry norms).
- Anchor the origin to a real problem the founder encountered that maps to the customer's problem — this is what makes the authority credible rather than decorative.
- Use the unique mechanism to explain why the product works differently, giving the founder or an expert the role of explaining the mechanism.
- Use the adversarial angle to differentiate: name what the industry does wrong and position the product against it.
- This framework suits founder-led brands where the founder has genuine category authority or a credible origin story. If the founder has neither, a witness or proxy format may fit better.
Caveat: The source frames this as a definition and architecture, not a guaranteed performance formula; adversarial claims should be truthful and substantiated.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, video-ads, founder-ads, positioning, hook-writing
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Highest-Performing Founder Script Structure
Use when: Writing or reviewing the body script for a founder-led video ad before shooting.
- Open with an adversarial claim that challenges the viewer's assumptions about the category (the source's example: 'the industry is lying to you'). This creates tension before the product is introduced.
- Immediately provide proof of that claim so the adversarial opening reads as credible rather than inflammatory.
- Introduce the unique mechanism tied to the founder's story — connect why the product works to why the founder built it.
- Back the mechanism with proof of mechanism: evidence, demonstration, or results that validate the explanation.
- End with a clear CTA plus social proof to close the loop on trust.
- Run this as the body script, then test multiple hooks in front of it rather than rewriting the whole structure per concept.
Caveat: This is presented as the source's highest-performing founder structure, not a universal law; it depends on having a real adversarial claim and real proof to back it.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, script-writing, founder-ads, hook-writing, creative-strategy
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Hooks Should Target Viewer Desire, Not Founder Narrative
Use when: Writing hook variants for a founder video ad or diagnosing why a founder ad has weak thumb-stop rate.
- Write hooks around the viewer's underlying desire or problem first, not around the founder's personal journey. The founder's story belongs in the body, not the opening line.
- Keep the body script stable and swap the hook to isolate the variable being tested. One body can carry several hooks.
- Test two to four hook variations per body script. Going much wider spreads the testing budget too thin to read a result.
- Evaluate hooks against the specific stage of awareness the ad targets — the same claim lands differently on problem-unaware versus solution-aware viewers.
- If a founder ad is underperforming on retention, check whether the opening is narrating the founder rather than the viewer's problem before rewriting the body.
Caveat: The two-to-four hook range is the source's testing heuristic, not a calculated threshold; adjust based on available spend and volume.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, experiment-design, creative-strategy, founder-ads
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Batch-Shoot Modular Creative for Volume
Use when: Planning a founder video shoot and deciding how many assets to produce per session.
- Break the creative into modular components: hooks and bodies shot as separate units.
- Use the source's arithmetic as a planning model: 10 hooks × 3 bodies = 30 ads.
- Shoot all hooks in one batch against the same lighting and setup, then shoot the three body scripts separately.
- In editing, assemble each hook with each body to produce the full matrix of ad variations.
- Continue swapping hooks as results come in without re-shooting bodies, which keeps marginal creative cost low.
- This approach suits founder-led brands that can get the founder on camera for a limited window; the shoot is planned around component coverage rather than finished ads.
Caveat: Volume of combinations is not the same as volume of distinct concepts; the source pairs this with concept diversification so the matrix is not just cosmetic variation.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, video-ads, production, experiment-design, founder-ads
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Concept Diversification via Founder Script Roles
Use when: Your founder ads all feel the same and you need distinct concepts without changing the offer or product.
- Default founder ads use the protagonist role: first-person, founder speaking directly about their own story.
- Diversify by changing the founder's script role: the witness (founder reads a customer email or message aloud), the interviewer (founder questions the lab or product team), and the reader (founder reads a one-star review).
- Each role changes the emotional entry point and the credibility mechanism without changing the product or offer.
- Treat role changes as concept-level variation, not hook-level variation — they should feel like different ads, not edits of the same ad.
- Match the role to the stage of awareness: a reader-of-a-bad-review format suits skeptical audiences, an interviewer format suits audiences who want mechanism proof.
- Distinguish this from superficial variation such as set or wardrobe changes when assessing whether the concept is truly different.
Caveat: Reads or reviews used in the witness or reader formats should be real and permission-appropriate; fabricated testimonials create legal and trust risk.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, founder-ads, video-ads, audience-awareness, concept-testing
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## The Founder Involvement Spectrum
Use when: Deciding how to involve the founder in the next creative batch, especially when shoot time or talent budget is limited.
- Place each creative on a spectrum of founder involvement rather than defaulting to full face-and-voice delivery.
- Protagonist: the founder on camera speaking in first person — highest authority, highest production demand.
- Voice: the founder narrates over product or b-roll rather than appearing on camera.
- Evidence: the founder appears indirectly, for example photos of the founder in the lab or facility.
- Proxy: an employee or other trusted person tells the founder's story on camera.
- Absent: no founder at all, told through animation or other formats.
- Use the spectrum to book shoot time efficiently — a single session can yield protagonist, voice, and evidence assets.
- Rotate across the spectrum to avoid format fatigue in the account without abandoning founder authority entirely.
Caveat: Lower involvement generally means weaker direct founder authority; the source presents the spectrum as range, not as a claim that outcomes are equal across levels.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, video-ads, production, creative-fatigue
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Editing Dynamics to Avoid Format Fatigue
Use when: Founder videos are still running but performance is declining and you need to refresh without changing the core concept.
- Introduce dynamic editing: multiple camera angles within a single video rather than one static talking-head shot.
- Change scenery and setting across different ads in the same concept family so they do not read as identical.
- Combine low-fi, UGC-style footage with high-end product shots to keep a premium brand positioning intact while retaining the authenticity of founder content.
- Use editing variation as a cheaper refresh lever than re-shooting; it can extend the life of existing footage.
- Track which combinations of angle, scenery, and edit style are running and rotate them instead of repeating the same edit across all placements.
Caveat: This reduces format fatigue; it does not fix a weak hook or a missing argument. Pair it with hook and concept variation.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, creative-fatigue, editing, production, founder-ads
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Match Founder Ads to Stage of Awareness
Use when: Mapping a founder ad set to cold versus warm audiences or diagnosing why a founder ad works in one placement but not another.
- Decide which stage of awareness each founder ad targets before scripting, since the same founder story does not land identically at every stage.
- For less aware audiences, lead with the problem and the adversarial claim so the viewer recognizes the problem before the product appears.
- For more aware audiences, the unique mechanism and proof of mechanism can carry more of the runtime, since the problem is already established.
- Reuse the same founder story assets across stages by altering which element is emphasized in the hook and opening, rather than rebuilding from scratch.
- Keep the target stage explicit in the creative brief so editors and media buyers are aligned on which asset belongs to which audience.
Caveat: The source identifies targeting specific stages of awareness as a component of founder ad performance; it does not provide numeric suppression or frequency rules.

Tags: paid-social-creative, audience-awareness, founder-ads, creative-strategy, targeting, video-ads
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder ads present an argument, not a founder monologue
Use when: Planning a founder-led video shoot or briefing a script for paid social acquisition creative.
- Treat the ad as the founder's argument, not a raw iPhone video of the founder: the format works because it carries authenticity, authority and trust from a real person, not because the founder is interesting.
- Do not stop at 'put the founder on camera': authenticity alone underperforms. The ad needs a scripted structure that delivers the argument.
- Write through the viewer's eyes: lead with an underlying desire or problem the audience already has, then use the founder story as the lens, not the subject.
- Show the viewer themselves in the story ('this happened to me too') and problem-agitate so they keep watching, open a curiosity gap around a solution, walk them through the mechanism, then close the loop.
- Before recording, confirm the script would still make sense if a non-founder delivered it — if yes, the founder may be optional and the script is doing the work.
Caveat: The source asserts nobody cares about the founder's story by itself; some founder-story-only ads do work, but the majority underperform.
Caveat: Do not invent a founder story you cannot stand behind — the source calls fabricated founder stories black hat and a risk to future exit value.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, scriptwriting, acquisition, audience-research
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Highest-performing founder ad script structure: claim, proof, mechanism, CTA
Use when: Drafting the body script for a founder video ad for cold acquisition traffic.
- Open with an adversarial claim that doubles as a hook, e.g. 'the industry is lying about X' or 'big supermarkets sell you Y that is full of plastic'.
- Immediately prove the claim — the source found no top performer that made a claim without proof. Example framing: 'a study found competitors contain 1/100 of the stated ingredient'.
- Move into the unique mechanism tied to the founder story: the adversarial problem existed, competitors did the bad thing, so the founder built the differentiated solution.
- Layer more proof onto the mechanism (factory visits, third-party testing, ingredient labels, certificates) so the viewer believes the mechanism is real.
- Close with the CTA — how to buy, the offer, scarcity and urgency — plus customer social proof.
- Keep the CTA simple: average watch time means few viewers reach it, so the source does not bother testing multiple CTAs on the same body.
Caveat: This structure was the top performer among the source's own top-spending founder ads in their ad account; it is a source-specific finding, not a universal ranking.
Caveat: Adversarial competitor claims carry compliance and legal risk — the source notes founders are often more willing than agencies to take that risk.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, scriptwriting, hooks, creative-strategy, cta
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Batch-shoot founder ads: pairing hooks with bodies
Use when: Scheduling a founder shoot and deciding how much creative volume to generate from one session.
- Prep distinct body scripts that tell materially different stories — not minor rewordings.
- Build a hook set for each body, then cross-combine: e.g. 10 hooks × 3 bodies = 30 ads.
- Optionally add a second CTA/social-proof ending to reach higher counts, but the source does not recommend testing CTAs since so few viewers reach them.
- Keep each hook contextually bridged into the body so the transition makes sense — mismatched hooks are a common failure in founder talking-head ads.
- Expect the actual recording time to be small (about 4–5 minutes of camera time for this volume) and put the effort into scripting and production, not filming.
- Scale volume by system, not headcount: the source reports clients producing 200–400 ads a month with one or two people on the content team.
Caveat: Large agency-scale volumes cited (hundreds of ads per month, 1–2 person teams) are the source's client examples, not a benchmark every store should attempt.
Caveat: Hooks that do not connect to the body break the ad even if they tested well in isolation.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, production, hooks, creative-strategy, batch-production
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder face is a sameness signal: diversify concept and format
Use when: A founder ad has started to fatigue and you are deciding what to shoot next.
- Diagnose whether the problem is format fatigue or concept fatigue: the same face, scene and talking-head delivery serving the same audience decays quickly.
- Attack format first because it is easy: reshoot the identical script in a new location. The source reports taking a fatigued in-gym ad and running another $40,000 of spend through it by only changing the scene.
- Stretch into new formats as volume grows: tier-list videos, founder walking a store aisle interacting with competitor products, or other organic-style formats instead of another talking head.
- Attack concept next by changing one of three levers: the persona being addressed, the angle/unique mechanism, or the offer.
- Note the source's difficulty ordering: offering a new or different product is easiest, changing the angle is hard because there may be only one true mechanism, and changing persona is hardest for founder ads because the founder must credibly represent that persona.
Caveat: The $40,000 incremental spend figure is one source example, not a repeatable benchmark.
Caveat: Reusing the same script is acceptable when editing or scenes vary substantially; the source frames this as true under current Meta conditions.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, ad-fatigue, concept-diversification, production
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Use portfolio theory: never let founder ads carry the whole account
Use when: Deciding budget allocation across creative formats after a founder ad becomes a top spender.
- Treat total creative production like a portfolio: no single ad or concept should become the whole business.
- Watch for the failure pattern the source describes — brands riding one founder ad with a few hook swaps to $20–30M, then hitting full audience saturation and decaying because they only produce founder ads.
- Keep VSLs, statics and animation/claymation formats running alongside founder ads so sales do not depend on one fatigued concept.
- Lean into a winner by making more variants, but maintain parallel output in other formats.
- Recognize founder ads as one of the highest-spend single-ad categories the source has seen, alongside VSLs — high reward with concentration risk.
Caveat: The $20–30M single-ad revenue figures are the source's own exposure to brands that collapsed after the ad fatigued, not an expected outcome.
Caveat: Founder ads are described as optional for large brands — not the bottleneck for many eight- to nine-figure businesses — but they can improve performance when executed well.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, budget-allocation, ad-fatigue, measurement
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder involvement spectrum: protagonist, voice, evidence, proxy, absent
Use when: A founder is camera-shy, time-constrained, or you need format diversification without new concepts for paid social.
- Top of the spectrum: on-camera talking-head founder, which is ideal but the most fatigue-prone.
- Voice-only: founder narrates over B-roll, animation or UGC footage — useful when the founder is uncomfortable on camera.
- Evidence: no speaking, but use founder photos in a lab, certificates, or a shot of them walking through the factory facing away from camera.
- Proxy: an employee, dietitian or supplier tells the founder story — less effective than the founder, but workable when the founder wants no face, voice or evidence involvement.
- Absent: animation/AI formats which can visually demonstrate the problem and mechanism (e.g. showing cells changing) while telling the same founder story end to end.
- Run the same script and concept across multiple levels of the spectrum to generate format diversification quickly.
Caveat: Effectiveness generally decreases as you move away from the founder actually telling the story.
Caveat: As you move toward proxy and absent, the format stretches away from being a true founder ad.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, format-diversification, production, animation
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Change the founder's role in the script to multiply concepts
Use when: You need new founder ad concepts without new founder stories, especially on smaller teams that cannot pay creators.
- Protagonist (most founder ads): first-person 'I built this because…'.
- Witness: first person but observed — 'a customer emailed me last week and said…'.
- Interviewer: the founder asks someone else (lab, manufacturing, design team) why they do something that benefits the customer.
- Reader: the founder reads a review aloud — e.g. a one-star review — scripted so it still walks through the buyer's stages of awareness.
- Mix this with other levers (persona, offer, proof type, stage of awareness) to reach roughly 20–30 distinct concepts from a small base.
Caveat: The source says role-switching is high leverage for six- and seven-figure businesses that want volume without paying creators, but less necessary on eight- to ten-figure brands where founder time is scarce and these ads can run on non-founders.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, scriptwriting, creative-strategy, concept-diversification, production
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder ad concept matrix: persona × awareness × angle × offer × proof × role
Use when: Building a new batch of founder ad concepts and deciding who each ad targets and what offer it carries.
- Variable 1 — persona/stage of awareness: e.g. skeptical first-time category buyer at problem awareness; existing category buyer at solution awareness; gift buyer at product awareness.
- Variable 2 — angle: for first-time buyers, 'the entire category is lying to you'; for existing buyers in dosed CPG, an incorrect-dosage angle; for gift buyers, a taste angle.
- Variable 3 — offer: single SKU for the first-time buyer; for the dosage angle, a bundle justified in the script because the second product makes the primary one work; a gifting pack for the gift buyer.
- Variable 4 — proof type matched to the angle: lab certificate for the first-time buyer, competitor teardown for the dosage angle, an email read for the taste/gift angle.
- Variable 5 — founder role: protagonist, witness, interviewer or reader.
- Then mix and match any single variable to create a new concept — swapping role alone gives four versions of each base ad.
Caveat: Stage-of-awareness changes also shift which proof and angle are appropriate, so do not change the persona label without adjusting the supporting proof.
Caveat: The specific examples (creatine, makeup, beef jerky) are illustrative; apply the variable logic to your own category.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, audience-research, offer-design, concept-diversification
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Static vs renewable founder story elements
Use when: Planning ongoing founder ad production and deciding what to source and script each month.
- Keep static (changing them costs credibility): the origin story, why the company started, and the founder's personal problem.
- Add value if you can: co-founder stories, including why the two founders' problems met in the middle — this multiplies available origin material.
- Treat as renewable: customer reviews and emails (they change weekly), the support inbox, supplier calls and interviews (relevant as products and suppliers evolve), one-star reviews, and ongoing day-to-day conversations or in-person complaints.
- Mine one-star reviews deliberately as script material — the source treats them as a constant for brands with meaningful volume and a format the founder can read on camera.
- Refresh ads by swapping only the renewable variables while holding the origin story consistent.
Caveat: Founder story material is finite: there is one true origin story with many ways to tell it, not an infinite supply of new stories.
Caveat: Fabricating or changing origin facts is called black hat by the source and a risk to exit value.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, scriptwriting, customer-research, creative-strategy, retention
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Run founder ad production as a collection pipeline
Use when: Setting up an ongoing founder ad cadence with limited founder time.
- Decide a monthly output target for founder ads as part of the broader creative mix (the source's illustration is 40 per month).
- Build collection systems: pipe reviews into a database, route the support inbox into an AI model you can pull weekly for ad-relevant language, and record or note supplier calls and comms.
- Capture one-star reviews, ongoing conversations and in-person complaints, and ask of each: could this become content?
- Store everything so scripting sessions start from collected raw material instead of a blank page.
- Shoot in concentrated blocks — once a month for general content, perhaps once a quarter with the founder — because the founder need not be on camera daily.
- The source reports shooting enough founder ads in a one- to two-hour block to cover three to four months.
Caveat: The 40-ads-a-month and one-to-two-hour shoot figures are source illustrations, not universal targets.
Caveat: This pipeline assumes someone owns collection and scripting; without that, concentrated shoots produce generic content.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, production, workflow, customer-research, creative-strategy
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder video production quality rules
Use when: Preparing a founder shoot and post-production for paid social creative.
- Do not upload an unedited talking head: use fast dynamic editing, overlays and engagement techniques that would retain viewers on organic content.
- If shooting a talking-head founder story, use two cameras and incorporate motion.
- Make every hook contextually relevant to the body — a hook about something that happened 10 years ago must connect to what the body actually discusses.
- If the founder struggles with a teleprompter, script only the sentence-by-sentence structure and let them speak off script so delivery feels natural.
- Change scenery as much as possible within the shoot window — even moving around one location adds immediate format diversification for the same script.
- Expect new founders to be robotic for roughly the first 10 hours on camera; the source treats this as a normal ramp, not evidence that founder ads do not work.
Caveat: The '10 hours' figure is the source's informal observation, not measured data.
Caveat: Founder ads shot without these production standards are a common reason brands wrongly conclude the format does not work.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, production, video-editing, hooks, creative-strategy
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Hook variation: 2–4 per body, up to 8, never 30
Use when: Deciding how many hook variants to launch per founder ad body in a paid social test.
- Set the natural limit first: only so many hooks can bridge naturally and contextually into a given body.
- Aim for 2–4 hooks per body as the working range; the source accepts up to about 8.
- Do not split a modest daily budget across 20–40 hook variants just to hit volume — the source warns that thin distribution prevents the ad set from exiting learning and gathering conversions.
- Balance example from the source: spreading $1,000/day across three ads tests better than spreading the same budget across 20–40.
- Reshoot hooks on existing winners, especially in new locations, to squeeze more spend from a fatigued high performer.
- Remember most viewers never pass the hook, so hook optimization is high leverage — a loser can become a winner with a hook swap.
Caveat: The source has run bodies with 40 hook variations and bodies with 9, so this is a practical sweet spot, not an absolute cap.
Caveat: The $1,000/day figure is an illustrative budget split, not a recommended minimum spend.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hooks, founder-ads, experiment-design, measurement, budget-allocation
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Run founder ads through the founder's handle as partnership ads
Use when: Choosing placement and ad account identity for founder video ads.
- Compare the same founder creative run through the company page versus a partnership ad through the founder's handle.
- Expect directionally better results from the founder's handle, but modest: the source cites roughly 5%–30% better performance, primarily driven by the founder's credibility and existing content.
- Check whether the founder's personal profile has relevant content posting regularly. The source's own ads sit at the high end of that range because the founder's page has high organic volume and frequent posting.
- If the founder's page has no relevant content (photos of daily life do not count), the lift is likely small and keeping ads on the company page avoids making the brand dependent on the individual.
- Weigh the trade-off: company-page ads build brand association; partnership ads mean customers may buy from the founder rather than the brand.
Caveat: The 5%–30% range is the source's firsthand experience, not a guaranteed uplift.
Caveat: The decision is described as nuanced and case-dependent, not always-run-through-the-founder's-handle.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, meta-ads, partnership-ads, measurement, acquisition
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder ads are optional, and your exit terms may care
Use when: Deciding whether to invest in founder-led creative or in other channels and formats.
- Do not treat founder ads as mandatory: the source says a $200M brand does not need the founder on camera to reach $300M, because creative format is unlikely to be the bottleneck at that scale.
- Consider founder ads anyway for general performance lift when executed well.
- Factor in cost structure: founder time is the primary production cost, which is favorable for small brands that would otherwise pay agencies, freelancers or creators.
- Use the founder story to differentiate commoditized products (fashion, commodity supplements) by repositioning around a specific persona and problem rather than competing on the mechanism everyone else sells.
- Flag exit risk: if founder ads hold a large share of spend, buyers may mark founder dependence in the term sheet — the source raises this as a real consideration, not a reason to avoid founder content.
Caveat: The source's claim that founder ads perform better when good is explicitly a generalization, not a universal rule.
Caveat: The exit/term-sheet concern is the source's characterization and varies by buyer and deal.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, positioning, acquisition, business-strategy
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Differentiate a commodity product through the founder's story
Use when: Developing ad angles for a commoditized product where every competitor advertises the same mechanism.
- Identify the saturated mechanism everyone sells (e.g. electrolytes, creatine), and avoid competing there directly.
- Build a specific persona-problem-story chain: who the founder was (e.g. a marathon runner), the specific problem they hit (dehydration), why existing solutions failed (XYZ), and therefore what they built.
- Aim the story at a narrow persona with a specific problem rather than the whole category.
- Use the founder's origin, mechanism and adversarial angle as the elements only the founder (or a credentialed expert) can credibly deliver.
- Layer customer experience and diverse persona callouts separately — the source argues the founder's own product praise is lower credibility because the founder is biased; customers who match the viewer's problem carry it better.
Caveat: This is the source's argument for why founder ads work, illustrated with hypothetical examples; it does not report controlled results.
Caveat: Calling the founder's story a 'mechanism' is the source's terminology — it acknowledges the story is differentiation, not a true unique mechanism.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, positioning, offer-design, creative-strategy, audience-research
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Decide Between Founder Handle and Company Page for Partnership Ads
Use when: Deciding which ad account/page identity to run founder creative from on Instagram/Meta for an ecommerce brand.
- Check content volume on both the founder's personal handle and the brand page: the source notes the page with more frequent, relevant posting tends to perform better because the ad inherits social proof from that profile's content.
- Judge relevance, not lifestyle: the source stresses the personal page must have founder-store-relevant content, not just photos of the founder with a dog at the park — non-relevant content gives little lift.
- If neither page has relevant content, the source says default to running through the company page: it builds brand association and avoids the risk that customers buy 'you' rather than the brand.
- Weigh the efficiency trade-off: running as a partnership ad through the founder handle can give an efficiency boost, but it makes sales depend on the founder, which the source flags as a risk for brands that want to be bought for the company.
- Treat this as a per-brand decision, not a fixed rule; the source explicitly says it is not one-or-the-other for everyone and that for many people keeping it on the same page is fine.
Caveat: This is the presenter's situational recommendation, not a universal rule; there is no benchmark for how much lift a personal handle delivers.
Caveat: Platform partnership-ad mechanics and eligibility change — verify current requirements in the ad platform interface.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, meta-ads, account-structure, brand-association, creator-partnership-ad
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

## Three Elements of the Dishwashing-Sheet Founder Ad
Use when: Writing or storyboarding a cold-audience founder ad for a consumable ecommerce product.
- Open with a negative or apologetic hook (the example: 'I need to publicly apologize to anyone who's ever bought these dishwashing sheets before, because I lied') combined with fast pacing so the opening does not read as an ad.
- Show the product working within the first three seconds — the source says this hooks the viewer by proving efficacy immediately alongside the hook.
- Put the founders front and center in the marketing so the brand reads as founder-led, which the source argues builds immediate trust because they put their reputation on the line.
- Lean into an underdog narrative: in the example, two brothers from a small market taking on large incumbents associated with harsh chemicals — a positioning the source says almost any small business can adopt by telling its story.
- Build the story and positioning into the creative itself rather than leaving it only on the brand's About page.
Caveat: The source presents this as an example of a winning ad, not a measured causal test; treat as a pattern to test, not a guarantee.
Caveat: Showing the product in the first seconds conflicts with other examples in the same source (see the natural-deodorant ad, where the presenter preferred holding the product back) — the right choice depends on awareness stage and format.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, hooks, positioning, cold-audience
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Use Format Innovation to Stop the Scroll
Use when: Planning creative concepts for cold-audience ecommerce acquisition when standard founder-to-camera formats are fatiguing.
- Audition formats, not just scripts: the source's example is a story-based ad shot professionally where the speaker talks to a crowd in a store, and the shots are rarely on the speaker's face.
- Use novelty to create a curiosity gap — the presenter reports being hooked specifically because he had never seen ad content in that format, which made him want to know what story was coming.
- Pair the unfamiliar format with a genuine story: the source says whenever you can weave a story into ad creative it performs better, and that the most obvious version is a founder story but it does not have to be one (the example tells the origin of a specific product).
- When structuring the edit, consider holding on the speaker longer: the presenter identified rapid cutting away to product shots and B-roll (some with out-of-sync audio) as the weakness, because each cut reminded him he was watching an ad.
- Test an alternate cut that removes that B-roll or delays the product until at least roughly the 20-second mark.
Caveat: The '20-second mark' suggestion is the presenter's personal preference, not a tested threshold.
Caveat: The claim that story-based ads 'always' perform better is the source's opinion — no comparative data is given — so validate on your own account.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, founder-ads, hooks, video-editing, experiment-design
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Sequence an Ad as Hook, Curiosity Gap, Objection Handling, Benefits
Use when: Scripting a founder product ad for an ecommerce brand, especially a supplement or food product with a common objection.
- Open with a credibility hook that signals legitimacy — the example opens with the product having taken over two years of development.
- Immediately open a curiosity gap (the example: 'and here's why') to pull the viewer forward before explaining anything.
- Insert social proof visually as the founder walks through the warehouse, so the audience sees scale/operations while listening.
- Bridge into objection handling: the example names every negative attribute of competing products ('gluggy, gritty, grainy, and gluey') before making any claim about his own.
- Move to benefits instead of features — smooth and creamy, under 1g of sugar, budget-friendly — after objections have been cleared.
- Name the product only after the problem and benefits are established, and keep the CTA from opening new curiosity loops the ad then never closes (the source criticizes 'forget everything you know about pea protein' because it bridges nowhere).
Caveat: The source flags the re-opened curiosity gap and the CTA as weaknesses in this otherwise strong ad — do not copy them.
Caveat: No performance numbers are given; this is a structural commentary, not a tested result.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, scriptwriting, objection-handling, hooks
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Problem-Agitation Arc Ending in Scarcity
Use when: Writing acquisition creative for a food or supplement brand entering a category with entrenched but flawed incumbents.
- Open with a contrarian claim about the category ('the protein industry is broken') paired with fast-paced graphics to hook attention.
- Agitate the problem against the viewer's daily reality and their ideal self, naming the specific persona ('you want more muscle... but every protein snack you see is ultra-processed').
- Extend agitation to competing solutions — the example dismisses beef jerky as soy, sugar and low-quality minced meat and not even high in protein, then notes typical jerky isn't made for lifters — so the viewer has no good alternative left.
- Introduce the founder story here: he designed something from steak, salt and spice; he quit his job, posted daily for a year, grew to over 100,000 followers and shipped across his country.
- Close with scarcity placed after the founder story ('I sell out all the time, but right now I've got stock'), then the CTA to get delivery to the door.
Caveat: This is a case-study narrative of one ad the presenter rates highly; the follower count and shipping claims are the brand's, not benchmarks.
Caveat: Agitation must be factually defensible — the source does not instruct making unsupported negative claims about competitors.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, problem-agitation, hooks, scarcity
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder Story Scripting from Personal Problem Onward
Use when: Structuring a long-form founder origin story for a product brand, even at large scale, and especially when AI-generated creative is flooding the feed.
- Start with the founder's own specific problem in first person and concrete physical detail (the Step One example: hiking, chafing, big legs, wearing bike shorts, sweating).
- Move into self-solutioning as relatably scrappy as possible — the example cuts up one pair of bike shorts and sews panels just between the legs, then notices the result.
- Walk through how materials and design decisions were reached (testing bamboo as a fabric, adding elastic around the pouch so it lifts, separates and supports) — the source calls this scripting 'really good' because each step follows the previous.
- Note that the brand built connection and a human presence, which the source says is especially valuable now that AI creative is flooding ad accounts.
- Close with risk reversal and social proof — in the example, a 30-day wear-and-if-you-don't-love-them-they're-free guarantee and 'join over 2 million customers' — plus a direct link CTA.
- Know that production value is not the constraint: the source notes this ad was shot on an iPhone, likely scripted in 5–10 minutes, walking around a park.
Caveat: The source's claim that a big $100M-scale brand running its first founder ad means founder ads are 'crushing for them' is the presenter's inference, not verified data.
Caveat: The 30-day guarantee and 2 million customers are that brand's facts, not templates for other brands.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, scriptwriting, storytelling, social-proof, guarantee
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Match Ads to the Stages of Awareness
Use when: Allocating creative concepts across prospecting and retargeting campaigns, or diagnosing why an ad with good engagement isn't scaling.
- Diagnose each ad by what the viewer must already know to understand it. The source's example: a hemp/rosehip oil ad opens with testimonials about how good the product is — that only works for people already product aware.
- Recognize the failure mode: for unaware audiences the ad introduces the product immediately with no problem education and no top-of-funnel agitation, so it can't resonate.
- Follow the source's stated allocation heuristic: put most ads in awareness stages one, two and three, with about 20% in stages four and five.
- Assign the product-aware testimonial-style ad to bottom of funnel, where it can convert people who just need a few video testimonials to tip them over.
- Before buying large cold reach behind any ad, confirm it contains enough educational or problem-framing material to make sense to someone who has never heard of you.
Caveat: Treat the 20% split as the source's heuristic, not a calculated benchmark.
Caveat: The source reports the oil ad had generated about half a million dollars in revenue to date — a single case example, not a promise for similar creative.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, awareness-stages, funnel, cold-audience, media-buying
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Founder Story Built on a Discovered Ingredient
Use when: Building acquisition creative for a supplement or ingestible brand with a novel delivery format.
- Open with a hook and a matching visual that are genuinely unusual (here, the founder citing the quantity of breast milk/colostrum bought so she could make the first gummy of its kind), then promise the explanation ('you're wondering how I got here, let me explain').
- Tell a concrete transformation story: the founder's mother had long-term gut issues, took colostrum, and reported gut lining repair plus hair and skin benefits.
- Pair the subjective family claim with objective proof by pulling in scientific studies — the source notes this pairing as the reason the claim lands rather than reading as pure anecdote.
- Name the real consumer friction that justifies the product ('powdered colostrum is hydrophobic, so you choke down a chalky mess') so the gummy format has a reason to exist.
- Establish supply-chain credibility with specifics: eight months of work, a named farm producing high-grade first-day-milking whole colostrum, a gummy manufacturer that preserves integrity, and the first 5,000 bags produced.
- End with a next-step CTA (follow to get first access) rather than an immediate hard sell.
Caveat: Health-outcome claims made by founders require your own regulatory/compliance review; claims in the example are not validated by the source.
Caveat: No performance metrics are given for this ad.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, supplements, social-proof, creative-strategy, hooks
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Sequencing Proof Before Product Reveal
Use when: Creating acquisition video for a premium-positioned product where you want to justify the price before showing the product.
- Open by building authority through the founder's credentials (time spent in dental labs) and then state a category-level claim the big brands 'hope you never notice'.
- Build stakes with a concrete, quantified research story: they bought every top-rated electric toothbrush, spent thousands of dollars on name brands with celebrity ads and $300 price tags, and used them for months.
- Agitate on the discovered problems — porous plastic absorbing bathroom moisture, charging bases acting like Petri dishes collecting black mould where the brush is stored, Bluetooth apps added to justify markup — before any product is shown.
- Reveal the solution only after agitation, with specific materials and spec choices (aircraft-grade aluminum vice plastic, 14 motor frequencies tested to settle on 32,000 micro-vibrations per minute, wall mount instead of countertop stand, 60-day battery).
- Use the contrast deliberately: the source notes low-fi founder footage early does not damage a premium brand as long as the product is not introduced in that lower-quality footage — it creates contrast with the later high-end product shots.
- Close on the positioning line and a comparison CTA inviting the viewer to upgrade from a name brand that stopped caring.
Caveat: The claim that low-fi footage is safe 'as long as the product isn't introduced at the same time' is the presenter's reasoning, not a tested result.
Caveat: Volume of proof claims must be legally defensible; the source gives no guidance on substantiation.

Tags: paid-social-creative, founder-ads, creative-strategy, product-reveal, premium-positioning, objection-handling
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

---

## Diagnose Bottom-Funnel-Only Creative Portfolios
Use when: Auditing a brand's existing creative library before scaling spend or when evaluating a celebrity/founder-fronted ad account's structure.
- Audit the library for awareness-stage spread: the source's example brand runs ads that read as bottom-of-funnel because they open with testimonials, taste, and value framing only meaningful to someone already sold.
- Check whether the ad assumes viewers know the spokesperson — the source says anyone who doesn't already know the figure will not watch that far, so reach is capped at the existing audience.
- Look for objections that belong at the point of purchase being used as acquisition messages: 'it tastes great' and 'cheaper than buying all the pills separately' are, per the source, close-only objection handling, not cold-audience value propositions.
- Ask the cold-audience questions the ad must answer: what is in the product, why should I care about any of those ingredients, who is this person, and why should I trust them.
- If a few bottom-funnel ads run alongside strong top-of-funnel creative, that's fine — the problem is when the entire library sits there and the account can only capitalise on the founder's existing audience.
Caveat: This is the presenter's diagnosis of an example library, without account data; do not treat it as proof about any specific brand's efficiency.
Caveat: Some brands deliberately concentrate on an existing fanbase — the source treats that as a reason scaling will stall, not as inherently wrong.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, awareness-stages, media-buying, account-audit, scaling
Source: How To Create Founder Ads That Scale (Masterclass).docx

## Hook objective: qualify viewers, not just grab attention
Use when: Writing or auditing the opening 3-5 seconds of a paid social video ad for a product page or direct-response campaign.
- Define the objective of the hook as qualifying and pulling in the specific target demographic, not maximizing raw attention.
- Reject curiosity gimmicks that attract everyone regardless of product relevance — e.g. stabbing a product with a knife to open a curiosity gap. This grabs indiscriminate viewers and feeds bad optimization data to the Meta targeting algorithm.
- Instead use a persona call-out hook that names the exact problem and audience, such as 'GLP-1 hair loss hits hard' or 'Your sauna sessions are burning off your hair.' These immediately filter viewership to the target demographic.
- Ask: does the hook make a relevant viewer think 'this is about me' or only 'what is going on?'
- Check that the hook's implied awareness stage matches the stage the rest of the ad actually serves.
Caveat: Source acknowledges a counter-argument: a visible product on screen (e.g. a protein shaker) might filter viewers by interest — but the presenter doubts this actually happens in practice.
Caveat: Curiosity hooks can still work as part of a larger qualified message; the problem is curiosity without qualification.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, meta-ads, targeting, awareness-stages
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Match the hook's awareness stage to the ad's body stage
Use when: Diagnosing why a video ad has high attention but low conversion on a store's Meta campaign.
- Classify the hook's intended audience stage: top-of-funnel curiosity hooks target problem-unaware or broadly problem-aware users; persona hooks target a specific problem-aware segment.
- Classify the ad body: listing features and ingredients is bottom-of-funnel, product-aware content; education about the problem and mechanism is upper-funnel.
- If the hook is broad and top-of-funnel but the body is a product tour, the hook pulls in people who aren't ready for the pitch and the ad mismatches its own serving stage.
- Decide the stage you want to serve (top, middle, bottom) and align both hook and body to that one stage.
- The Magic Mind example: a knife-into-product curiosity hook introducing the product by the third word, followed by ingredient and feature education, is a bottom-funnel product ad using a top-funnel hook — the presenter attributes the mismatch to weak performance.
Caveat: Presented as the source's analysis of specific examples, not a guaranteed performance rule.
Caveat: Bottom-of-funnel product ads can perform well with product-aware audiences; the issue is only the mismatch with the hook.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, awareness-stages, meta-ads, ad-troubleshooting
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Write a bridge that delays the product pitch
Use when: Restructuring a video ad script where the product pitch arrives too fast after the hook.
- Identify the bridge: the transition from the hook into the educational or problem-agitating body of the ad.
- Avoid hard-selling the product inside the bridge. Introducing the product immediately after problem agitation feels too aggressive and tends to underperform.
- Extend the education by a few lines before the pitch. In the FU2 creatine gummy example, the presenter suggests adding a line about the founder doing due diligence and reaching out to international suppliers before revealing the product.
- In the IM8 gut example, the presenter suggests continuing to educate on prebiotics and postbiotics before bridging to 'one supplement instead of several' — an extra four to six seconds of education.
- Confirm the bridge explains the mechanism or 'why the product works' before naming it, so viewers arrive at the product with intent rather than being sold to mid-epiphany.
Caveat: The source explicitly says these are hypotheses from single examples; brand results are illustrative, not promised.
Caveat: Delaying too long may lose viewers who are already product-aware and want the offer.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, script-structure, conversion-optimization, awareness-stages
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Attack problem-agitation and objection handling, not just features
Use when: Writing the body of a video ad or product-page copy where the audience needs to understand why the product works.
- Translate every feature into a benefit in the viewer's life — e.g. 'velvet suede material' becomes 'taking a nap at work.'
- Agitate the problem against an enemy: in the NAD ad, injections are positioned as painful, expensive and inconvenient, making the oral alternative the obvious conclusion.
- Hand a strong objection: the shaker example handles 'I bought a metal one and it was still plastic,' reducing the addressable market but deepening relevance with buyers who experienced that.
- Stack social proof and credibility for the solution, e.g. referencing a 12-week clinical trial with reported percentages (85% better digestion and less bloating; 95% more energy).
- Check that the benefit still lands for someone who does not do the activity (e.g. creatine benefits for sleep-deprived, brain-health viewers rather than only gym-goers).
Caveat: Trial figures in the example are the brand's claimed results and specific to that ad, not general benchmarks.
Caveat: Objection handling that is too narrow (e.g. 'you bought a plastic metal shaker') can shrink your total addressable market.

Tags: paid-social-creative, messaging-strategy, video-ads, objection-handling, copywriting, social-proof
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Add dynamic editing to prevent drop-off on static talking heads
Use when: Reviewing or editing a talking-head video ad that is underperforming on hold rate.
- Treat lack of movement as a primary drop-off driver; viewers scrolling feeds will skip a static shot in favor of a real.
- Add overlays, B-roll and rapid cutting — the source recommends cutting roughly every 3 seconds at minimum.
- Add basic motion even without a full edit: the presenter suggests him holding the camera and pulling it in and out.
- Compare the creatine gummies ad (heavy overlays and editing, reportedly around 20 times more engaging in the source's assessment) against the shaker ad (good script, static talking head) to see the gap.
- Improve the script as well, but recognize that a good script delivered with no motion can still fail.
Caveat: The 'cut every 3 seconds' figure is a source recommendation, not a universal benchmark.
Caveat: '20 times more engaging' is the presenter's subjective comparison of two example ads.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, video-editing, creative-production, retention, meta-ads
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Use a multi-persona call-out to widen the top of funnel
Use when: Writing the opening line of a video ad for a broad-appeal product like a supplement.
- Replace a single narrow condition call-out with a list of related conditions. The IM8 example opens with 'You have IBS, leaky gut, or bloating' — hitting three personas at once.
- Note that if the ad had said only 'leaky gut', most viewers would have self-excluded.
- After the call-out, reinforce that the problem is common and undiagnosed, e.g. '40% of people have it, most don't even know.' This increases the perceived addressable market and viewer self-identification.
- If you name a clinical term, immediately explain why the viewer probably has it; the source warns that naming 'dysbiosis' and moving on loses people who don't recognize it.
- Keep the call-out language specific to the target demographic so it does not read as generic.
Caveat: The 40% figure comes from the example ad's claim, not an independent statistic.
Caveat: Broadening too far weakens relevance; the source's point is to widen within the product's true market.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, messaging-strategy, audience-research, customer-research
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Don't shrink TAM with overly narrow brand positioning
Use when: Deciding the target persona and positioning for a supplement or wellness product page and ad messaging.
- Estimate the true addressable market before committing to a persona. The source argues the main market for supplements is sedentary people, the majority of the population.
- Sell benefits that apply without the gym: sleep deprivation support, brain health, cellular energy and general wellbeing.
- Avoid 'not for the everyday sedentary crowd, this is for serious athletes' positioning because it excludes the largest buyer group and makes a large business unlikely.
- Account for the operational friction of the elite athlete segment: coaches oversee what athletes take and there are more compliance restrictions.
- Verify any health claim you use is compliant; the source itself flags uncertainty on some claims.
Caveat: The source's claim about TAM size and scalability is the presenter's opinion, illustrated with a specific Australian creatine brand example.
Caveat: Compliance rules vary by market; check claims before publishing.

Tags: paid-social-creative, positioning, messaging-strategy, audience-research, supplements, market-sizing
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Define niche terms so credentials don't backfire
Use when: Writing product education for a specialized audience where incorrect terminology risks credibility.
- Check your ingredient list for redundancy or inaccuracy before publishing. The source points out an ad claiming 'three ingredients: electrolytes, magnesium and creatine' while magnesium is itself an electrolyte.
- If the target audience is elite athletes, assume they know the science and will spot the error.
- Match vocabulary precision to the audience: the more specialized the persona, the less forgiving the copy.
- Apply the same check to disease names and mechanisms — if you say NAD decline causes symptoms, be ready to explain why, or the claim reads as unsupported.
- Use plain terms when possible; the presenter notes the NAD ad under-educates on what NAD is and therefore fits solution-aware audiences rather than problem-aware ones.
Caveat: The source calls its own critique 'nitpicky' but argues precision still matters to the target audience.
Caveat: This is a copy-accuracy principle, not a compliance guarantee.

Tags: paid-social-creative, messaging-strategy, copywriting, product-education, audience-research, positioning
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Edit out AI-scripting giveaways, especially contrast negation
Use when: Reviewing a script drafted by an AI tool before producing a video or writing product page copy.
- Scan the script for contrast negation: statements like 'Your gut isn't broken, it's starving' or 'Your gut isn't broken, it's not X, it's Y.'
- Add an explicit instruction when prompting AI copywriters: 'Do not use contrast negation.' The source recommends this as a standing rule.
- Look for other AI tells noted in the source, such as overused analogies like 'sending a single soldier into a war zone' and hyperbolic scientific framing that reads as generic.
- Rewrite the flagged lines into concrete, specific claims that keep the message but sound like a person talking.
- Ask whether the line survives a viewer's skeptical ear; the presenter says such lines are a 'huge giveaway' that the ad is AI-scripted.
Caveat: This is the presenter's stylistic judgment; some contrast-negation lines may still work in tested ads.
Caveat: Compliance and tone still need separate review from AI-tell removal.

Tags: paid-social-creative, copywriting, script-structure, ai-tools, video-ads, quality-control
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Leverage a credible figure and a native setting for authenticity
Use when: Planning a founder-led or celebrity-led video ad creative for a product or landing-page hero video.
- Open with a known figure to borrow general credibility, even when they are not a category expert — the NAD example uses a Transformers actor.
- Include a viral or in-demand keyword in on-screen text, e.g. 'peptides' and 'NAD+', to create curiosity for the unfamiliar term.
- Shoot in a native, organic-looking setting such as a car rather than a pro studio. The source notes that a three-camera lighting-rig setup immediately signals 'this is an ad.'
- Use conversation or interview framing so the hook reads as content rather than a commercial.
- Weigh the compliance risk of using a famous person's image and voice; the source flags uncertainty and says the brands doing it seem to manage it.
Caveat: Using a public figure's likeness may carry compliance or rights risk — the source is unsure and says to verify.
Caveat: Celebrity credibility is general, not category-specific; viewers may not see them as an expert on the product.
Caveat: The source calls the example's script 'sloppy' even though it performed well.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, creative-strategy, influencer-marketing, native-content, social-proof
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Reinforce relevance after naming a condition
Use when: Writing product education about a health problem or a technical product benefit that viewers don't already know they have.
- State the condition or mechanism in plain language.
- Immediately add prevalence and invisibility framing: '40% of people have it, most don't even know.' This does double duty — market size and self-diagnosis.
- Avoid dropping a term like 'dysbiosis' and moving on; the source says viewers will wonder what it means and assume it does not apply to them.
- Follow the term with observable symptoms the viewer recognizes so the framework attaches to their experience.
- In the IM8 example, the ad then explains the cascade (good bacteria die, bad bacteria take over, inflammation, leaky gut, poor nutrient absorption) before referencing the product.
Caveat: Prevalence claims in the example come from the ad itself and were not independently verified.
Caveat: Health claims and disease references may be regulated; confirm compliance for your market.

Tags: paid-social-creative, messaging-strategy, video-ads, product-education, copywriting, awareness-stages
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Structure a VSL's first 30-40 seconds as hook, problem, mechanism
Use when: Scripting the opening of a long-form VSL used on a landing page or as a paid social placement.
- Open with a persona call-out, ideally covering multiple related personas to widen the top of funnel.
- Move into problem agitation: what the viewer has tried, why it failed, and what their doctor or previous solutions did not address.
- Introduce the mechanism or reframe the cause before naming the product — the IM8 example goes from gut problems to bacteria balance before the product.
- Continue the education into the product's mechanism (prebiotics, postbiotics, how probiotics colonize) before bridging to the offer.
- Stack credibility next: clinical trial percentages, nutrient counts and multiple reported benefits, then close.
- The source also suggests keeping the middle flowing and not making the bridge into the product too aggressive.
Caveat: The 3-4 minute length is described as typical, not mandatory.
Caveat: Trial and nutrient figures are the brand's claims from the example ad.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, vsl, script-structure, landing-page, awareness-stages
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Build a swipe file of competitor ad libraries for creative research
Use when: Sourcing new ad angles, hooks or formats for your store's paid social creative pipeline.
- Identify brands consistently shipping strong direct-response creatives in your category — the source names IM8 as one to watch.
- Study hook mechanics, bridge construction, editing density and problem agitation in their ads rather than just the products advertised.
- Note how long each ad has been running and its visible engagement as rough signals of whether it is working.
- Adapt the structural pattern to your own product; do not copy claims or product efficacy commentary.
- Use findings to brief new scripts and edits rather than as finished creative.
Caveat: Engagement counts and run time are weak proxies; the source uses 'three likes over a couple of weeks' as an informal signal, not a measurement system.
Caveat: The source notes ad performance metrics, ROAS, scaling and attribution belong in a separate domain, not creative analysis.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, competitor-research, meta-ads, hook-writing, video-ads
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

## Diagnose underperformance by segmenting hook, bridge and body
Use when: Troubleshooting a video ad that has low conversion or low engagement after launch.
- Watch the ad in three segments: hook (first 3-5 seconds), body education, and the bridge between them.
- Score the hook: does it qualify the right persona, or just grab attention?
- Score the bridge: does it introduce the product too early or too aggressively?
- Score the body: does it educate, handle objections and translate features into benefits?
- Connect the diagnosis to the specific segment rather than rewriting the whole ad. In the FU2 example, the source says the hook and early education were strong and suspects the aggressive bridge caused the underperformance.
- Keep the strongest segment and test a revised version of the weakest.
Caveat: The FU2 attribution to the bridge is the presenter's inference from low engagement over a couple of weeks, not a controlled test.
Caveat: One ad's run time or like count is not a reliable performance metric.

Tags: paid-social-creative, ad-troubleshooting, video-ads, hook-writing, experiment-design, creative-strategy
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Adapt hooks and education to the audience's awareness stage
Use when: Choosing whether a given ad should target problem-aware or solution-aware audiences in a Meta campaign.
- Classify the ad's implied audience stage by how much the body explains.
- Problem-aware audiences need education on what the mechanism is and why it declines — the source says the NAD ad under-explains what NAD is and why it drops.
- Solution-aware audiences already know the mechanism; they need reinforcement, differentiation and the offer.
- If the audience is problem-aware, add the missing 'why' before the pitch or expect the ad to underperform with that group.
- Use the stage classification to decide campaign placement rather than forcing one ad to serve every stage.
Caveat: Stage labels are the source's analytical framing, not measured segments.
Caveat: The source's assessment of which stage an ad fits is interpretive.

Tags: paid-social-creative, awareness-stages, video-ads, audience-research, messaging-strategy, meta-ads
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Meet the numbers you promise in ad claims and copy
Use when: Finalizing claims, offers and supporting numbers for a video ad or product page.
- Check every numeric claim in the script against a source you can defend — the examples include '82% of users' from a clinical study and 'up to 16% off.'
- Keep offer claims simple and specific; the shaker ad's 'up to 16% off today' is a dated offer that needs correct dates and terms.
- If you cite a trial, state what it measured; the IM8 ad reports 85% better digestion, 95% more energy, 80% better sleep.
- Remove unsupported disease or efficacy claims unless they are clearly compliant in your market.
- Review the final script against the product page so the offer the ad promises is redeemable at checkout.
Caveat: All figures above come from the example ads and are the brands' claims, not verified results or benchmarks.
Caveat: The source explicitly admits uncertainty about compliance on some claims.

Tags: paid-social-creative, offer-design, compliance, copywriting, product-page, trust
Source: How To Make A Winner Meta Video Ad.docx

---

## Clarity vs. Curiosity: Pick One, Never the Middle
Use when: Writing or auditing the headline and primary text of a static ad for a cold-audience acquisition campaign.
- Decide the strategic direction of the ad before writing: choose either (A) explicit clarity — the reader instantly knows the product, the mechanism and the offer (e.g. 'fasting window hack'), or (B) deliberate curiosity gap — an intentionally incomplete statement that forces a click to resolve (e.g. 'Symptoms of vitamin deficiency' presented as a native post).
- Test the copy against the middle-ground failure mode: vague slogans like 'You don't have to feel ready. You just have to begin' cause confusion, not curiosity, because nothing is promised or withheld in a way the reader cares about. If the reader's reaction is 'I don't get it, I don't care', rewrite.
- When choosing curiosity, make sure the gap is one the audience actually wants closed. AG1's 'We don't stop improving. Neither should you' opens a gap nobody cares about — the audience has no stake in the brand's improvement.
- When choosing clarity, anchor it with specificity, since a concrete number builds instant authority (the source cites '96% of users are more productive' as an example — note the source's caveat that specificity reads as scientific backing even when the claim is invented, which is why you must only make claims you can substantiate).
- If the ad is curiosity-led, route the click to an educational advertorial landing page that closes the gap; sending a curiosity click to a standard product display page is a stated primary reason top-of-funnel statics fail.
- Keep the format matched to the choice: native/tweet-format statics naturally suit curiosity, clean product-hero statics suit clarity.
Caveat: The source presents this as a heuristic, not a tested rule; treat the clarity/curiosity split as a copywriting lens, not a guarantee of performance.
Caveat: Specificity only builds authority if the claim is true and legally defensible — the source's remark that invented stats still read as credible is an observation, not a licence to fabricate claims.

Tags: paid-social-creative, static-ad-design, messaging, creative-strategy, conversion-optimization, hook-writing
Source: How To Make Better Meta Ads_ 8 Static Creatives Analysed.docx

---

## Design Static Ads Around the Eye's Reading Path
Use when: Laying out a static ad image and deciding what element goes where to maximise cold-audience attention and comprehension.
- Place the single strongest attention magnet first — usually a headline in large type or a striking background image that stands out against the feed. The source's best-in-class example (a protein coffee drink static) led with a bold centre headline plus an unusual background image.
- Use the image to open a question the headline then answers (or vice versa): the reader should think 'what is this drink?' and the headline '2-in-1 mocktail and protein fix' should resolve it. This image-headline tension is what generates the click.
- Close the curiosity gap immediately with one bold, concise line of explanation in the centre — don't make the reader hunt through the ad to learn what the product is.
- Add short supporting bullets only for information needed to understand the product; the reference ad used 'grass-fed Aussie whey, 100% real coffee and chocolate'.
- Finish with social proof and a clear product visual so the buyer is confident the product will look like what's advertised — the source compares this to avoiding a 'McDonald's photo shoot' gap between ad and reality.
- Audit the failure mode: awkward spacing, an oversized logo, confusing graphs or sub-headlines too small to read on an iPhone break this flow and destroy authority. Enlarge unreadable supporting text by roughly 30% in the source's estimate and shift the product to free layout room.
Caveat: This is design craft guidance drawn from deconstructing eight statics, not a measured benchmark; the 30% enlargement is a presenter's suggested fix, not tested data.
Caveat: The source's claim that the reference static would 'flop' without its background image is an opinion about that specific ad, not a general law.

Tags: paid-social-creative, static-ad-design, visual-hierarchy, creative-strategy, product-page, conversion-optimization
Source: How To Make Better Meta Ads_ 8 Static Creatives Analysed.docx

---

## Treat Image and Headline Like a YouTube Thumbnail and Title
Use when: Pairing a photo or illustration with a headline for a static ad, or diagnosing why a static is getting impressions but no clicks.
- Write the headline and select the image as one unit, asking what extra context the image adds that the headline doesn't state. The source's example: 'this one simple switch made taking creatine 10x more enjoyable' only works because the photo shows a person holding creatine gummies, revealing that the 'switch' is gummy format.
- Run the substitution test: swap the image for a generic equivalent (a tub of creatine powder, or a plain bag labelled 'creatine') and re-read the headline. If the reader can no longer tell what the 'switch' is, the pairing is too loose to perform.
- Reject saturated, generic visuals when the headline depends on novelty — the source notes a plain creatine powder shot would fail both on saturation and on lack of contextual fit.
- Let the headline do objection-handling and exaggeration in few words: 'this one simple switch' handles the difficulty of changing routine; '10x more enjoyable' raises the perceived benefit and pushes the reader to the landing page for education.
- Keep a retargeting layer in mind: the static can carry a light clickbait load if the landing page educates and follow-up ads continue the story.
Caveat: Illustrated with a single ad example; the source does not provide click-through or sales data for it, so treat the pairing test as a diagnostic heuristic.

Tags: paid-social-creative, static-ad-design, headline-writing, creative-strategy, hook-writing, landing-page
Source: How To Make Better Meta Ads_ 8 Static Creatives Analysed.docx

---

## Match Static Ad Format to Stage of Awareness
Use when: Choosing which static ad concepts to produce for a cold prospecting campaign versus a retargeting campaign.
- Classify each concept by Eugene Schwartz awareness stage before producing it: most-aware/product-aware statics (plain product shots, 'guaranteed energy in 5 minutes') belong in retargeting, while unaware/problem-aware audiences need statics that lead with education or a named problem.
- For cold audiences, open with a problem the viewer recognises rather than the product: the GLP-1 hair loss static leads with 'GLP1 hair loss hits hard' and only then introduces the supplement.
- Use advertorial-style statics (native statics that look like content) to educate before the hard sell, since they let you agitate the problem without immediately demanding a purchase.
- Check the mismatch symptom: a clean product shot with a benefit headline running against cold traffic is the format-audience mismatch the source names as the primary reason top-of-funnel statics fail.
- Route each stage to the right destination — education/curiosity concepts need an educational landing page, while product-aware concepts can go to a standard PDP.
Caveat: Awareness stages are used as a strategist's organising framework here; the source offers no measurement data linking stage to ROAS, so treat the alignment as a planning discipline rather than a proven lift.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, awareness-stages, messaging, acquisition, funnel-mapping
Source: How To Make Better Meta Ads_ 8 Static Creatives Analysed.docx

---

## Build Top-of-Funnel Statics Around Universal Problem Lists
Use when: Designing a broad-reach cold-audience static for a supplement, wellness or consumable product where you need reach without wasted impressions.
- Format the static as something the audience already recognises as non-advertising — the IM8 example uses a tweet screenshot, which the source frames as a format arbitrage opportunity while the format is still unfamiliar in feeds.
- Lead with an educational core headline that opens a curiosity gap and self-selects the right audience: 'Symptoms of vitamin deficiency' only appeals to people who care about that, which filters the click.
- List problems that most of the population experiences at some point (fatigue, low energy, itchy or dry skin, bruising, painful muscles) and label each with the corresponding nutrient the product contains — the source estimates the IM8 list addresses roughly 70% of the general population.
- Drive the reader into the primary text for the offer rather than relying on an in-ad call to action; the source argues a convincing ad doesn't need an explicit CTA because readers take the next step themselves.
- Run the static through a partner or creator handle rather than only the brand handle, and note partnership ads work as statics, not just video.
Caveat: The '70% of the general population' figure is the presenter's impression of that ad, not a measured stat — do not reuse it as a benchmark.
Caveat: The claim that ads need no CTA is the source's opinion, illustrated by one scaling ad; it does not override your own testing or platform requirements.

Tags: paid-social-creative, static-ad-design, native-formats, awareness-stages, acquisition, creative-strategy
Source: How To Make Better Meta Ads_ 8 Static Creatives Analysed.docx
