## Meta Creative Team & Production Benchmarks by Monthly Ad Spend
Use when: You're setting the creative org, output volume and budget split for your store's Meta ad program and need a scale-appropriate structure rather than copying a big brand's setup.
- At 4–30k/month: founders shoot the content themselves, an iPhone is fine, and you aim for a natural look. Limit yourself to three core concepts done well — the leverage is in script quality, not concept count. Allocate a high proportion to testing — roughly 60–80% of budget — because you lack the spend to scale winners yet. Use trial reels as a cheap way to validate ideas before committing production.
- At 30–100k/month: bring on two to three UGC creators, direct about 25% of spend to production, expand to five to six concepts to start de-risking, and produce roughly 30–100 new creatives per month. Add a scaling campaign and begin introducing partnership (whitelisted/creator) ads.
- At 100k+/month: stand up a full creative team — a scripter, an editor, four to six creators and a designer (part-time is acceptable). Commit a minimum of ~10% of ad budget to creative, run 8–10 active concepts, and produce at least 100 new assets per month (the source's rule of thumb of roughly $1k of spend per ad).
- The source's framing: at 100k+ the thing you're actually buying is a system that outputs creative continuously — without it, creative becomes the permanent bottleneck.
Caveat: These spend bands and ratios are the presenter's heuristics, not measured benchmarks — recalibrate against your own account.
Caveat: The 100-assets figure is explicitly derived from a 'basic $1k per ad' formula, so it scales with spend rather than being a fixed target.

Tags: paid-social-creative, meta-ads, creative-strategy, team-structure, scaling, production, ugc
Source: The Creative Strategy Behind $100M_yr of Meta Spend.docx

---

## Splitting Production Budget Between Iteration and Net-New Concepts
Use when: You're allocating a paid-social production budget or briefing a creator/editor team on what to make next month.
- In the production budget, deliberately weight the majority toward iteration on concepts that have already shown signal (new hooks, new opens, different angles on the same idea) rather than net-new concept design.
- Treat net-new concepts as the smaller share — they seed the pipeline, but the source states iteration is what drives most performance.
- Operationalise this by keeping a roster of proven concepts and versioning them (swapping the hook, the first three seconds, the visual treatment) before spending on fresh ideas.
- Pair this with the stage-appropriate concept count so you don't spread iteration too thin across too many parent ideas.
Caveat: This is the source's stated allocation principle; the exact split is not given as a hard number, so treat 'majority' as directional.
Caveat: Requires a feedback loop — if you aren't tracking which ads are winning, you can't know what to iterate on.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, production, iteration, budgeting, meta-ads
Source: The Creative Strategy Behind $100M_yr of Meta Spend.docx

---

## Tracking Cost-per-Asset and Per-Ad ROI to Justify Creative Spend
Use when: You're deciding whether to keep or cut a creator, agency or content format, or need to defend creative budget to a founder or CFO.
- Stand up a financial model that records cost per asset broken out by source (which creator, which agency, which internal team produced it).
- Pair that with average profit contribution per ad from that source, so you can compare whether a more expensive creator's content actually earns its cost back.
- Evaluate estimated contribution after product and variable order costs, media spend and allocated production costs. Media spend divided by production cost is not ROI.
- Use the output to shift budget toward content sources whose cost per asset is justified by profit contribution, and away from ones that aren't.
- This is presented as the 100k+/month standard, but the same logic can be applied at smaller scale with simpler tracking.
Caveat: Attribution of profit to a single ad is inherently noisy; treat per-ad ROI as directional.
Caveat: Requires discipline in tagging production costs to the assets they produced — without that tagging the model is meaningless.

Tags: paid-social-creative, measurement, creative-strategy, unit-economics, meta-ads, roi, budgeting
Source: The Creative Strategy Behind $100M_yr of Meta Spend.docx

---

## Trial Reels and Partnership Ads as Cheap Creative Validation Levers
Use when: You're at the top of a creative testing cycle and want to know which concepts or creator partnerships are worth scaling before committing full budget.
- At the 4–30k/month stage, use trial reels as a low-cost way to validate a concept before putting real production or media budget behind it.
- At the 30–100k/month stage, begin introducing partnership (creator/whitelisted) ads as a way to branch out and de-risk — you're leveraging the creator's handle and audience rather than only your own account.
- Route validated winners out of the trial/partnership layer and into your scaling campaign.
- Keep the validation layer running continuously so you always have a pipeline of pre-vetted concepts feeding the scaling campaign.
Caveat: Trial reels and partnership-ad features change over time — verify the current interface and eligibility rules in Meta Ads Manager before relying on them.
Caveat: Validation performance on trial placements may not perfectly predict performance at scale.

Tags: paid-social-creative, meta-ads, testing, partnership-ads, trial-reels, creative-strategy, de-risking
Source: The Creative Strategy Behind $100M_yr of Meta Spend.docx

---

## Letting Creative Volume, Not Pre-Optimisation, Reveal What Good Looks Like
Use when: You're tempted to over-plan creative before launching, or you're deciding how much to invest up front in perfecting scripts and concepts.
- Accept the source's claim that for most advertisers creative is the binding constraint on Meta — media buying is no longer where the leverage is.
- Instead of trying to predict winners, build the loop: generate concepts, script them, get ads into the account, read the feedback, and iterate.
- Use your stage-appropriate concept and asset counts (three concepts at small spend, five to six mid, eight to ten at scale) as the input to that loop, not as a one-time launch exercise.
- Protect the volume: the bottleneck at scale is almost always whether the creative machine keeps producing, not whether any single ad is perfect.
- If you find yourself constantly scrambling for creative, treat that as the diagnosis that the production system — not the individual ad — is what needs fixing.
Caveat: 'Volume solves it' is the presenter's opinion; quality and concept relevance still matter, and low-quality volume burns budget.
Caveat: Small accounts have limited spend, so volume must be matched to the testing allocation (60–80% at 4–30k/month) rather than maximised blindly.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, testing, meta-ads, iteration, feedback-loop
Source: The Creative Strategy Behind $100M_yr of Meta Spend.docx

## Two-Format Creative System: Top-of-Funnel Problem/Solution and Bottom-of-Funnel Objection Ads
Use when: Deciding what creative concepts to brief for a paid social campaign, so you stop wasting production budget on vague middle-funnel ads.
- Split all creative into two formats: top-of-funnel problem/solution ads and bottom-of-funnel objection-handling ads.
- For top-of-funnel, address the core problem the avatar has and position your product as the unique solution. Problems can be functional (back pain) or identity/emotional (clothing makes you feel cool, part of something).
- For bottom-of-funnel, make one ad per objection (price vs. competitor, warranty/expiration, delivery speed, quality doubt). Show one specific proof point per ad rather than dumping all benefits.
- Accept that longer, hook-heavy top-of-funnel ads will naturally bleed into the middle of the funnel; shorter or simpler versions of either format cover the middle-bottom gap.
- Do not build dedicated middle-funnel creative unless you have a specific reason.
- Run catalog/DPA retargeting to anyone who viewed similar products as the non-creative bottom-of-funnel layer.
Caveat: The source presents this as the currently working approach in the 'Andromeda era'; funnel economics shift as platform algorithms change.
Caveat: Skipping middle-funnel creative is a deliberate trade-off relying on natural spillover; some categories with long consideration cycles may need explicit middle creative.

Tags: paid-social-creative, funnel-strategy, creative-strategy, objection-handling, top-of-funnel, bottom-of-funnel, meta-ads
Source: The ONLY 2 Ad Creative Formats You Need to Scale Facebook Ads.docx

---

## Creative Hit Rate: Measure Winning Ads Instead of Total Ads Launched
Use when: Evaluating whether to increase ad production volume, or auditing why a high-volume creative testing program is underperforming.
- Define a 'high-performing ad' upfront using two criteria: it spent more than 5% of total campaign budget AND hit your target CPA.
- Calculate hit rate = (number of high-performing ads) / (total ads launched) × 100.
- Interpret it: launching 100 ads with 5 winning ads is a 5% hit rate, described by the source as a solid, fairly normal benchmark. Volume negates luck, so 1,000 ads at the same hit rate implies ~50 winners.
- Before producing more ads, ask whether each new ad has a genuine chance to be a winner. Only scale volume if the baseline quality holds.
- When volume rises but hit rate falls, treat that as a signal you are diluting quality, not scaling.
Caveat: The 5%-spend threshold and 5% hit rate are the source's own definitions and heuristics, not industry standards.
Caveat: Increasing volume only compounds if hit rate is stable; otherwise you multiply low-quality spend.

Tags: paid-social-creative, measurement, creative-strategy, testing, meta-ads, roas
Source: The ONLY 2 Ad Creative Formats You Need to Scale Facebook Ads.docx

---

## Avoid Flooding the Pixel With Low-Quality Creative That Creates Negative Events
Use when: Deciding whether to bulk-upload thousands of AI/UGC ads, or diagnosing declining account performance despite rising spend.
- Recognize the mechanism: the pixel fires, sends signal events to Meta, and delivery optimizes based on the purchase event to find similar buyers at lower CPA.
- Understand that the pixel also trains on negative events — bounces, carts not converted, unqualified traffic — not just purchases.
- Realize that flooding an account with thousands of low-quality ads disperses budget and generates negative events that mis-train the pixel and degrade targeting accuracy.
- Before scaling creative count, vet each ad for its likelihood of attracting the right avatar; reject ads likely to pull unqualified clicks even if they are cheap to produce.
- Treat the pixel's recent event stream as a quality signal to protect, not just a volume number to grow.
Caveat: This is the source's explanation of Meta pixel training, not a documented platform spec; algorithm behavior changes and should be re-verified in the current interface.
Caveat: The source does not provide a quantified threshold for how many negative events cause harm.

Tags: paid-social-creative, algorithm-mechanics, meta-ads, pixel, conversion-optimization, measurement
Source: The ONLY 2 Ad Creative Formats You Need to Scale Facebook Ads.docx

## Avatar-and-Angle Framework for Top-of-Funnel Concepts
Use when: Generating top-of-funnel ad concepts and creative briefs for a product, especially when stuck for angles.
- Define one avatar (your ICP) at a time — the specific person you are selling to.
- List every problem that avatar has, including problems not obviously related to your product; each problem becomes a potential ad angle.
- Combine one avatar with one angle to create a concept/packaged ad.
- Write ads that explicitly call out the avatar and their problem, then position your product as the solution.
- Note the source's claim that the algorithm tends to show ads mentioning a specific group to that group (e.g., an 'if you're ex-X' ad reaching ex-X people), reinforcing the need for explicit avatar callouts.
- Repeat across multiple avatars who can buy the same product for different purposes — e.g., a greens product framed for GLP-1 weight-loss users vs. for hair growth.
Caveat: The algorithm-matches-avatar behavior is the source's claim about the Andromeda era and may not hold universally or persist.
Caveat: Listing unrelated problems can dilute relevance; the source frames it as a brainstorming step, then you select the strongest angles.

Tags: paid-social-creative, customer-research, creative-strategy, positioning, avatar-framework, top-of-funnel, meta-ads
Source: The ONLY 2 Ad Creative Formats You Need to Scale Facebook Ads.docx

---

## Mine Bottom-of-Funnel Objections by Calling Checkout Abandoners
Use when: Building bottom-of-funnel retargeting creative or diagnosing why add-to-cart does not convert to purchase.
- First, write down every objection to buying your product that you already know (price, shipping, trust, warranty).
- Second, call customers who literally abandoned their checkout and ask why they did not purchase. The source calls this the best method for surfacing true bottom-of-funnel objections.
- Convert each surfaced objection into its own ad: price objection → value/quality proof or competitor comparison; warranty objection → show the guarantee; delivery objection → show speed.
- Keep each bottom-of-funnel ad focused on one objection and one proof point, not a full feature list.
- Example from source: a hair product ad stating a single clinical claim, and a cookware ad leveraging a lifetime warranty to counter competitors' limited warranties.
Caveat: Abandoned-checkout calls require customer contact data and may have low response rates; the source presents it as high-signal but does not quantify outcomes.
Caveat: Results of example ads are illustrative, not guaranteed benchmarks.

Tags: paid-social-creative, objection-handling, customer-research, bottom-of-funnel, retargeting, creative-strategy
Source: The ONLY 2 Ad Creative Formats You Need to Scale Facebook Ads.docx

## Structure the Ad Account as Avatar/Angle 'Packs' Inside One CBO Campaign
Use when: Setting up or restructuring a Meta ad account so you can read results and control spend by avatar, angle and funnel stage.
- Create one core prospecting campaign (e.g., 'ML Prospecting') using auction buying and a Sales objective.
- Set daily budget to where you are currently comfortable — the source warns not to use budget as a lever to increase spend, and suggests a rough floor of at least 1× your target CPA.
- Choose bidding (highest volume or value) and keep budget scheduling at 'none'.
- Set audience segment reporting: engaged segments = site visitors last 30 days and add-to-cart last 90 days; existing customers = 180-day purchasers (not all-time).
- Name each ad set as a 'pack' with a sequence number plus avatar and angle, and label the funnel position (top or bottom).
- Exclude existing customers from the prospecting campaign; leave placements wide open; select the correct dataset and the purchase conversion event.
- Use a standard attribution setting (source uses 7-day click, 1-day engaged-view, 1-day view). Do not use dynamic creative or live video ads.
- Launch each new batch of ads into a new pack. You can run dozens of packs in one campaign — the source reports accounts with 30–100 ad sets — which allows control via value rules, minimums and maximums instead of pausing ads.
- Expect the pattern over time: top-of-funnel packs spend more and scale further at lower ROAS; bottom-of-funnel packs convert better but scale less.
Caveat: Menu names (buying type, performance goal, attribution windows) change as Meta updates the interface — verify current settings.
Caveat: Performance goal choice: maximize conversions for single-product or equal-price brands, value of conversions when prices vary.
Caveat: The 1× target CPA daily budget is a rough starting heuristic, not a rule.
Caveat: Packs beyond the core prospecting campaign (retargeting, retention, scale campaign) exist but the source does not detail them here.

Tags: paid-social-creative, campaign-setup, meta-ads, account-structure, cbo, ad-sets, measurement
Source: The ONLY 2 Ad Creative Formats You Need to Scale Facebook Ads.docx

## Don't Do This If You Like This hook
Use when: Writing the opening 1-3 seconds of a TikTok/Reels creative for a consumable or habit product (appetite suppressant, snack, supplement, beauty routine) where you need to stop the scroll before pitching.
- Frame the hook as a restriction on something the target buyer already loves: 'don't drink this if you like eating' — the audience's love of food creates the relatability, the restriction creates the curiosity gap.
- Cast a creator who visibly embodies the tension (e.g. someone who describes themselves as 'a foodie') so the premise reads as personal experience rather than a scripted claim.
- Deliver the payoff through first-person storytelling ('if I don't eat before I drink this I'll forget to') rather than a feature list; the product is the implied resolution, not the opening subject.
- Treat this as a three-purpose asset: post organically, hand to affiliates, and run as a paid ad — the same file can serve all three.
- Match price to impulse range for TikTok: the source cites examples around $29-$39 as a range that still leaves margin.
Caveat: Source is one practitioner's breakdown of TikTok Shop examples; cited view/sales figures are individual case results, not benchmarks.
Caveat: The 'don't do this' construction can read as fear-baiting if the restriction is not genuinely connected to the audience's desire.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, hooks, creative-strategy, video-ads, affiliate-content
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Problem/Solution comparison video with competitor callouts
Use when: Producing creative for a product in a category crowded with lookalike competitors (phone accessories, cleaning tools, kitchen gadgets) where buyers need a logical reason to switch.
- Open by calling out the problem in the viewer's own words ('if you see a lot of dust...'), then immediately show two competing products failing — they break, scratch, or misalign.
- Demonstrate your product doing the exact tasks the competitors failed at, on camera and in one take, so the comparison is visible rather than asserted.
- List multiple competitor reasons-to-leave and multiple of your benefit-reasons-to-buy so the difference is unambiguous.
- Use conditional logic scripts to move the viewer toward self-identification: 'if you're experiencing X problem you need this; if you're using X product and not seeing X result you should use this.'
- Build the demo as its own setting (a tabletop, a bathroom counter) rather than cutting between clips — the source argues better demonstration correlates with more sales than raw view volume.
- Note the source's example: high views (tens of millions) did not automatically produce proportionally high sales — demonstration quality mattered more.
Caveat: Calling out named competitors may expose you to platform ad policies and comparative-advertising rules; verify current TikTok ad policy before running as paid.
Caveat: The source's 'over-exaggerated' problem visuals (implausibly dirty phone) are his example — overstating a problem can damage trust if the audience finds it implausible.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, creative-strategy, video-ads, competitor-positioning, demonstration
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

## Transformation teaser: tease the result, don't reveal it
Use when: Creating branded creative for a results-based product (fitness drink, protein, skincare, body transformation) when you have no influencer and need a low-production asset for your own page.
- Write an on-screen or voiceover tease of the future state without showing it — the source's example is a countdown-to-vacation beach/bikini/abs line over a product shot.
- Highlight 2-3 key ingredients or specs visually (protein content, active compound) so the product reasons are present even though the outcome is hidden.
- Keep it short — the source's example is only a few seconds long — which makes it cheap to produce repeatedly at volume.
- Apply the same format to before/after content: show before and after, or show only the before with a telegraphed after.
- Publish it as branded creative on your own page; the source notes this format does not require an influencer or affiliate.
Caveat: The source asserts curiosity/controversy/mystery consistently outperforms straight product showcases — treat as his opinion, not a measured rule.
Caveat: Teasing a transformation invites skepticism; comment-reply videos are the follow-up mechanism he recommends for handling doubt.
Caveat: Cited view and sales figures (e.g. 203k views, ~$5k sales) are a single case example, not a forecast.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, hooks, creative-strategy, video-ads, branded-content
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

## Reaction-style video: bottle the response on camera
Use when: Producing creative for products with a sensory or dramatic payoff — supplements, flavors, taste tests, nasal sticks, clothing transformations, before/afters.
- Identify whether your product produces a sensation or visual change 'a magnitude higher than normal' — the source says the format works only where the reaction is genuinely strong.
- Set a one-take scenario: street interviews, gym taste tests, or a person trying the product for the first time; the source's example opens with 'two, one, take a deep breath' before the reaction.
- Capture the unfiltered physical or facial response rather than describing the product; the viewer's curiosity about what they don't yet know (what it smells like, feels like) drives questions and sales.
- Use the reaction as the hook and place product explanation and CTA after it, keeping the back end explicit.
- Expect this format to also build the account itself — the source credits this strategy alone for driving follower and view growth.
- Extend the format to a 'what you wear now vs. what you should be wearing' comparison for apparel, capturing the person's reaction to the change.
Caveat: The source's agency client growth claim is a self-reported case example, not a guaranteed outcome.
Caveat: Reaction content can feel staged; using real first-time participants is what makes it land.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, video-ads, creative-strategy, ugc, demonstration
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Misconception video: use a credible source to reframe a belief
Use when: Writing creative for a category with entrenched wrong beliefs or low awareness of the mechanism (sleep, breathing, skincare, nutrition) where you need to create demand for a solution buyers don't know exists.
- Open by naming the belief the audience holds ('mouth breathing during sleep is harmless') and then contradict it with a specific, credentialed source or study reference shown or spoken in the video.
- Spell out the consequence the audience didn't know: the source cites a Stanford study example contrasting mouth-breathing and nose-breathing twins and claimed facial-structure differences.
- Only after establishing the misconception, introduce your product as the fix — a sleep mouth tape being worn during sleep to change the outcome.
- Anticipate pushback: the source says this format generates questions in the comments, which is an asset — reply with comment-reply videos and link to the product from there.
- Reuse the format for 'if you always believed you should do X, you're actually wrong' framings aimed at viewers who currently choose a competing behavior.
Caveat: Claims of scientific consensus must actually be accurate; using a credible source is the mechanism, and misattributing studies will backfire in comments.
Caveat: The source's example generated comparatively modest sales (~3,400) despite decent views — this format builds audience and questions more than immediate volume.
Caveat: The presenter's use of an attributed study is summarized here as his example; verify any specific study before citing it in your own creative.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, creative-strategy, video-ads, positioning, customer-research
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Comment-reply video: turn audience questions into creative
Use when: Finding your next winning creative angle, or answering the objection that keeps appearing in your TikTok/ad comments (does it actually work, how does it stay on).
- Scan comments for a question or disbelief statement with visible likes and replies — that is the demand signal, and replying to it re-serves the video to everyone who engaged with that comment.
- Open the video visibly replying to the comment, then demonstrate how the product works in a visual, sensory way rather than explaining it abstractly ('they grip to your body heat, not sticky').
- Show the before/after or the mechanism directly so the skeptic sees it resolved on camera.
- Link directly to the product from the video and reuse the asset as a paid TikTok ad to scale it beyond organic reach.
- Accept that some comment replies go viral for the wrong reasons — the source argues the algorithm generally routes it to the right audience anyway.
Caveat: The social-proof lift from replying to a liked comment is the source's stated mechanism; treat as his observation.
Caveat: A video that goes viral for unintended reasons can still spend budget inefficiently — monitor who is actually converting, not just view volume.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, creative-strategy, social-proof, video-ads, customer-research
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

## Free tip / secret narrative that resolves into a product pitch
Use when: Writing creative for a supplement or consumable where buyers already live with a specific recurring annoyance (nighttime bathroom trips, poor sleep) and will watch a peer's story.
- Lead with the problem state in the viewer's voice — the source's example is waking multiple times a night to use the bathroom.
- Frame the answer as a tip or secret you were reluctant to share ('I actually just listened to my wife, she was spot on') so the opening reads as storytelling, not advertising.
- Reveal the tip is actually a product — the source's example is two products, one positioned as a nitric oxide/blood flow booster.
- Keep the video looking like raw, natural storytelling rather than a polished ad, and place the pitch in the middle-to-back half.
- Hook at the start, explain the product in the middle, and close with a clear call to action.
- Use the format as a template across organic, affiliate, and paid placements from one shoot.
Caveat: Health-adjacent claims (blood pressure, circulation) require compliance review before use as a paid ad — verify current platform and regulatory rules.
Caveat: The source's ~1.3M views and ~$22k sales are a single example, not a performance expectation.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, video-ads, creative-strategy, storytelling, supplements
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Hook-Value-CTA structure for short-form direct response
Use when: Structuring any TikTok/Reels creative for a product, especially when adapting an existing winning format to a new product or angle.
- Choose one hook format for the opening seconds — a reaction, a free tip, a 'don't do this' restriction, a misconception, or a problem callout.
- Deliver the middle as relatability or value: raw storytelling, demonstration, or breakdown of the problem, with the creator explaining the product inside that story rather than as a standalone pitch.
- Place the product reveal and call to action in the middle to back end, so the viewer is already engaged before the ask.
- Keep the whole thing looking non-ad-like: the source emphasizes every reviewed video is positioned to feel relatable and native rather than produced.
- Ship each asset simultaneously to three channels — organic post, affiliate content, and paid ad — so one production serves reach, affiliate sales, and scaled spend.
Caveat: The hook framing is the source's opinion on what drives view volume; test hooks against your own audience rather than assuming one dominates.
Caveat: The 'use one video in all three channels' workflow depends on affiliate program terms and ad rights — confirm permissions before repurposing creator content as paid.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, creative-strategy, hooks, video-ads, affiliate-content
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Bundle strategy to raise AOV when unit price is too low
Use when: Pricing or merchandising a low-ticket product (the source's example is ~$4.99) where a single unit cannot carry ad spend, or when planning bundles for a consumable.
- Diagnose whether the single-unit price can support paid acquisition — if not, don't simply raise the unit price; consider stackable multipacks instead.
- Create 3-pack, 5-pack, and 6-pack options that lift AOV to roughly $25-$30 in the source's example.
- Present the bundle as the default purchase path so the buyer's decision is pack size, not whether to buy.
- Check that the bundle makes sense for a consumable replenishment cycle so repeat purchase and AOV move together.
- Combine with a video format that already sells on curiosity (misconception, free tip, reaction) so the creative drives to a higher-value offer.
Caveat: The specific $4.99 -> $25-$30 figures are the source's example of what other sellers were doing, not a validated benchmark for your product.
Caveat: Multipack pricing must still show a credible per-unit saving versus single units or buyers will comparison-shop against it.

Tags: paid-social-creative, pricing, bundles, offer-design, aov, ecommerce-merchandising
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Match creative format to product type
Use when: Planning a creative testing slate for a new product launch or deciding which format to shoot first when budget or shoot time is limited.
- If the product produces a strong sensory or emotional response on first use, start with reaction-style — the reaction itself is the hook.
- If buyers currently use a flawed competitor product, start with problem/solution competitor comparison so the switching logic is visible.
- If the product has an aspirational outcome that takes time (fitness, body, skin), use a transformation teaser rather than a product showcase.
- If the category is full of false beliefs, use a misconception video with a credible source to create demand, then position the product as the fix.
- If the product is a consumable tied to a recurring annoyance, use the free-tip/secret narrative; if it looks visually surprising in use, use comment-reply demonstration.
- If the product has a strong desire-based restriction (eating, drinking), use the 'don't do this if you like this' hook.
- Shoot each selected format with the hook-value-CTA structure and distribute to organic, affiliate, and paid.
Caveat: These pairings are the source's practitioner heuristics from TikTok Shop examples, not tested universal rules — validate with your own creative testing.
Caveat: A single product can support multiple formats; treat this as a starting slate for testing, not a permanent assignment.

Tags: paid-social-creative, tiktok, creative-strategy, creative-testing, video-ads, product-selection
Source: The TOP TikTok Ad Creatives That Convert (STEAL THESE).docx

---

## Don't Abandon Hook Testing Because of Andromeda
Use when: Deciding whether to keep testing hooks on a fatigued winning ad or to kill it and start entirely new creative concepts.
- Recognize the widespread claim that Andromeda groups minor variations into single Entity IDs and therefore punishes hook testing; the source argues this leads advertisers to a mistake.
- Understand that Andromeda groups visually and contextually identical ads, not every variation of a hook; the source maintains hook testing still works on live accounts.
- Agree that diverse creative has always been valuable, but don't let that reasoning cancel the hook's outsized importance in paid social video.
- Because the hook sells the ad, the source places roughly 90% of a creative's impact in the first 3-5 seconds, citing Ogilvy's headline logic.
- Continue running hook variations on proven bodies as your default low-effort, high-ROI play rather than only producing entirely new assets.
- Verify current Meta/Andromeda behavior on your own account rather than assuming the algorithm has made hook testing obsolete, because tool behavior can change.
Caveat: This is the source's opinion and account experience, not a documented platform rule; confirm with your own tests.
Caveat: Andromeda grouping behavior may change over time, so re-validate the assumption on live campaigns.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, meta-ads, andromeda, creative-strategy, video-ads
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Revive a Fatigued Hero Ad by Replacing Only the Hook
Use when: A previously top-performing video ad (e.g. testimonial) has fatigued after extended spend and stopped scaling.
- Confirm the ad is genuinely fatigued: in the source's example a top performer that ran roughly six to seven months and was about 5x better than anything else began to lose results.
- Instead of filming entirely new concepts, rework only the first 3-5 seconds of the proven body.
- Avoid simply repurposing more hooks from the existing footage; most of your market already saw that hook and dropped off, so reuse of the same avatar/visual rarely re-engages them.
- Shoot new hooks with a different person, e.g. EGC (Employee Generated Content) delivering the hook before transitioning into the original CGC (Customer Generated Content) testimonial body.
- In the source's case this restored performance to prior levels at the same daily spend, and the reworked ads became the account's current top performers.
- Treat these results as one account's example, not a guaranteed outcome; test your own winner before scaling spend assumptions.
Caveat: The source could not show the actual client ad, so the performance figures are uncorroborated.
Caveat: Results cited (5x, six-to-seven-month fatigue, restored spend) are specific to one account and not benchmarks.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, creative-strategy, ad-fatigue, egc, ugc
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Four Levers for Varying a Hook: Angle, Offer, Avatar, Format
Use when: Planning hook variations for a proven ad body and choosing which dimensions to change.
- Lever 1 – Angle: change the messaging or contextual/seasonal framing. In the source's example the hook was made contextually relevant to the current time of year so an older-sounding testimonial felt current again.
- Lever 2 – Offer: change the CTA or product focus. This often lives in the copy or headline rather than the spoken hook; e.g. pivot from a single product to a bundle or a sale.
- Lever 3 – Avatar: change who delivers the hook. Using an EGC character with a very different profile from the on-screen testimonial hooks new people the original avatar missed.
- Lever 4 – Format: change the first 3-4 seconds visually. Options include a 3-second static or GIF/motion-graphic intro with a written testimonial overlay that transitions into the video.
- You don't need to change all four: a single lever (the source changed angle and avatar) can revitalize the ad; changing all four can also work.
- Pair lever changes with volume because results depend on testing enough variations.
Caveat: Combining all four levers is described as very likely to work but only 'through volume', meaning it requires producing many variations.
Caveat: Changing only wording will re-hook some viewers but not everyone; a visual change is also needed for broader re-engagement.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, creative-strategy, angle, offer-design, avatar, ad-format
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Test More Than Three Hooks to Beat the Odds
Use when: Choosing how many hook variations to produce when revitalizing a proven winning ad.
- Reject the trap of limiting variations out of fear that Andromeda will group them all together; that fear can push you to under-test.
- If you test only an angle change and a couple of avatar changes (the source's example of three hooks), the odds of a high performer are low unless you are an exceptional scriptwriter with deep audience knowledge.
- Instead produce a higher volume of hooks on the proven body; in the source's example they ran four different hooks across two bodies, reworking both hooks and body slightly.
- Budget the work so the volume is achievable: making 10 versions of a 3-5 second hook is roughly the same effort as producing one ~75-second ad body, since most shooting and editing effort lives in the body.
- Because the hook drives retention, concentrate repeated low-effort iterations there while keeping the proven body intact.
Caveat: The claim that 'three hooks is usually too few' is the source's heuristic, not a calculated benchmark.
Caveat: The 10-versions-equal-one-body effort comparison is the source's practical estimate for their own 75-second format.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, experiment-design, creative-strategy, video-ads, creative-testing
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Why Hooks Deserve the Budget: Drop-off Math
Use when: Allocating creative production time and budget between hooks versus full ad bodies.
- Apply the source's premise that roughly 80-90% of viewers drop off before the ad body, making the hook the most critical variable in retention.
- Consequently, the source argues about 90% of advertising dollars are 'wasted' after the hook if the hook doesn't sell.
- Invest most iteration effort in the first 3-5 seconds because improvements there affect the largest share of your audience.
- Since producing multiple 3-5 second hooks is a fraction of the effort of full new ads, the source considers hook testing the highest-ROI creative activity.
- Note the tension acknowledged by the source: an extremely good single hook could outperform, but the odds of hitting one are low, which is why volume in hooks is preferred.
- Do not merely tweak scripts; combine script changes with angle, offer, avatar and format changes to reach new viewers.
Caveat: The 80-90% drop-off figure and the 90% effort allocation are the source's stated premises, not independently verified benchmarks.
Caveat: Treat the 'spend 90% of effort on the hook' framing as a directional principle, not a literal budget rule.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, creative-strategy, video-ads, retention, measurement
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Distinguish EGC Hooks from CGC Bodies
Use when: Structuring a testimonial ad where the hook and testimonial come from different sources to expand reach.
- Define EGC as Employee Generated Content, e.g. a team member or client-side person filming the opening on-camera hook.
- Define CGC as Customer Generated Content, i.e. the customer providing the core testimonial that carries the ad body.
- Build the ad so a new EGC hook (a different person from the original avatar) plays first, then transitions into the proven CGC testimonial.
- Use a deliberately different avatar profile in the EGC hook from the one in the body to hook viewers the original presenter never reached.
- Reuse the proven body unchanged underneath, since its performance is already established; only the 3-5 second intro is new.
- Pair EGC hooks with angle changes when the original body's wording or visuals feel dated.
Caveat: The EGC/CGC split is the source's terminology for this hybrid structure; it is not a platform-defined format.
Caveat: Source's client case is one example and results are not guaranteed.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, egc, ugc, creative-strategy, video-ads
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Raise the Quality Bar for Hook Variations
Use when: Diagnosing why new hooks fail on a proven ad and deciding whether to blame the method or the execution.
- When new hooks fail, don't conclude that hook testing no longer works or that Andromeda killed it; first check whether you reused existing footage rather than producing genuinely different hooks.
- A common failure pattern: taking hooks from the existing video and repurposing them so the majority of the market has already seen and rejected them.
- The goal is to re-serve the ad to the same audience with a differently delivered hook that gets them to hold and watch through.
- Recognize that a wording change alone re-hooks some viewers; add visual changes to reach those who still drop off.
- Assume the bar is higher now: unless you are unusually strong at scriptwriting and audience understanding, generic variations are unlikely to hit.
- Decide to increase production effort and volume (e.g. a new presenter, new context, new format intro) before concluding the approach is dead.
Caveat: The claim that 'the bar has gone up' is the source's qualitative observation, not a measured threshold.
Caveat: Failed hooks can be caused by poor scripts or mismatched audience, not only by algorithm changes.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, creative-strategy, experiment-design, andromeda, creative-testing
Source: Why Hook Testing Still Works With Andromeda.docx

---

## Frame-by-frame Meta video ad teardown workflow
Use when: Reviewing a live or draft Meta video ad for a product page traffic campaign and deciding whether to scale, iterate or kill it.
- Watch the ad in 3-second chunks and score seven structural elements: hook, bridge, feature-to-benefit mapping, objection handling, proof/authority, cut pace, and CTA. Missing any one is a reason the ad underperforms.
- Check the first 3 to 5 seconds for whether the hook attracts the right buyer for the right reason, not just any viewer. Confusion-based attention (a sound effect, an unexplained object) pulls a broad wrong audience.
- Find the bridge — where the hook transitions into education or problem agitation — and confirm it does not hard-pitch the product immediately. Ads that show the product within the first few seconds cause drop-off.
- Count whether cuts happen at least every 3 seconds. Continuous talking-head with no visual change (as the roofer ad did in its back half) reads as salesy and disengages viewers.
- Check whether every feature named is tied to a benefit, and whether objection handling appears before the CTA rather than being skipped.
- For partnership or creator ads, test whether swapping the creator's face would kill the ad; if it only works on the creator's existing audience, it cannot extend past organic reach.
- Fix the first half first. Only around 15% of viewers reach the second half of a video ad, so a strong back half cannot rescue a weak opening.
Caveat: The 15% second-half retention figure is the presenter's working estimate, not a measured benchmark; verify against your own retention curve.
Caveat: Scoring is qualitative; use it to prioritise fix order, not to predict ROAS.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, creative-strategy, hook-writing, conversion-optimization, ad-review
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Build hooks that select the right viewer
Use when: Writing or rewriting the opening 3 seconds of a Meta video ad for a cold audience where the previous hook had high views but poor conversion.
- Write the hook to grab the right people for the right reason. A hook that confuses (an unexplained visual or sound) gets attention but delivers the wrong audience and tanks downstream conversion.
- Test the hook against five criteria: clarity, relevance to the buyer, novelty, specificity, and credibility.
- Use high-stakes warnings that problem-agitate immediately — the sauna example opens with 'sauna sessions are burning off your hair' and then opens a curiosity gap about why and how to fix it.
- Use contrarian authority ('the food system is toxic') when you can back it with proof; the claim alone is a strong scoop but needs evidence later.
- Avoid product-first hooks. The Vitable ad opens with 'these are my vital packs' — no curiosity gap, no relevance, and no identifiable hook type.
- Always ask whether the hook reveals what the product category is. The roofing ad kept viewers confused about what was even being discussed until too late.
Caveat: High-stakes claims must be true for your product and market or they create refund and trust risk.
Caveat: A hook that works on an organic feed may not work as a paid cold-traffic hook; test in the paid placement.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, creative-strategy, audience-targeting, video-ads
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Delay the product pitch: the bridge principle
Use when: Scripting the 5 to 15 second section of a video ad where you currently introduce the product immediately after the hook.
- Map the bridge as the segment between hook and educational/problem-agitating body. Most weak ads break here by saying 'the solution is our product' and listing benefits.
- Instead of naming the product, state that a solution exists and teach why the problem happens. The sauna ad says the solution is simple and highly effective, briefly shows the hat, then explains that sauna dries the scalp, splits ends and weakens hair.
- Follow the explainer with objection handling before any hard pitch: 'It's not a hair mask. It's not a magic pill.'
- Leverage category-level authority instead of product-level differentiation. The sauna ad sells the entire category (the hat was invented thousands of years ago in Central Europe to protect hair) rather than its own brand's differentiators.
- If your ad currently opens with the product, rewrite only the first half; the back half (benefits, objection handling, CTA) is often reusable.
Caveat: Selling the whole category is powerful for a category-defining product but does nothing for a commodity where competitors are indistinguishable.
Caveat: Delaying the product only works if the educational segment itself is genuinely useful to the viewer.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, hook-writing, script-writing, creative-strategy
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Turn features into lifestyle benefits (and lead with the benefit)
Use when: Rewriting a UGC or demo ad script that lists product specs without explaining why the buyer should care.
- List the features you want to mention (e.g. velvet suede material, adjustable armrests, fold-out leg rest).
- For each feature, write the user-level outcome it enables. Velvet suede becomes 'it feels good and I can relax for a couple hours'; adjustable armrests become 'I can lie down and take a 30-minute nap during the workday'.
- Then flip the order: state the benefit first, feature second. Instead of 'feature, feature, feature, therefore benefit', say 'I can take a 30-minute nap in the middle of the day because the armrests go down and the material is velvet smooth.'
- Reason for the flip: you always lose viewers as the ad runs, so get the benefit to more people rather than the feature.
- Push features that are category table-stakes (e.g. plant-based, dairy-free, gluten-free protein) through quickly — anyone already in the category knows them.
- Keep at least one objection-handling benefit near the end, as the protein ad does by addressing the common belief that plant protein tastes bad.
Caveat: The advantage of benefit-first is presented on retention-curve logic (viewers only drop off); the source gives no measured performance comparison.
Caveat: Do not drop compliance-required feature statements just because they are table-stakes.

Tags: paid-social-creative, script-writing, messaging-positioning, video-ads, benefit-selling
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Match the CTA intensity to the ad format
Use when: Writing the closing line of a video ad, especially a creator or partnership ad, where a hard sales close would break the format.
- If the ad has done heavy education and objection handling, use a soft CTA. The sauna ad closes with 'your halo is waiting for you' because the consumer should already be sold.
- In a testimonial or partnership ad, keep the close in the creator's voice. The chair ad says 'you should definitely invest in a comfortable working experience', which fits the value proposition of an expensive daily-use item.
- Avoid a hard sales pitch bolted onto a soft, tonality-driven creator ad — it disrupts the format.
- Use time or scarcity framing only when it is real and consistent with the offer. The protein collab ends with 'grab this pack before stock runs out' tied to the limited licensing deal.
- Check that the CTA points to the specific bundle or offer you want to push, not just the brand.
Caveat: Scarcity framing must be truthful; the source notes the stock claim ran for 20 weeks.
Caveat: A soft CTA works when the ad has already converted the viewer mentally; on a weak script it just adds friction.

Tags: paid-social-creative, call-to-action, video-ads, script-writing, partnership-ads
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Borrow authority from licensing and category history
Use when: Selecting an angle for a cold-audience ad when your brand has no recognisable founder or following.
- Identify a licensed partner or collaborator with existing brand equity (the protein ad uses Kellogg's and a named collaborator) and lead with it to build authority in the first seconds.
- Alternatively, use manufacturing origin as a positioning asset: the chair ad uses 'designed and developed in Japan' to differentiate from a 'gamer boy' aesthetic and to signal premium quality.
- Combine the borrowed authority with a curiosity gap or a numbered structure ('three reasons why') so the ad has a reason to keep watching beyond the name.
- Make sure the borrowed authority is genuinely relevant to the buyer's belief system, not just a logo.
Caveat: Origin-based premium framing is culturally contingent; the source explicitly notes 'made in Japan' did not carry premium associations decades ago.
Caveat: Licensing deals end — plan what happens to the creative when the partnership expires.

Tags: paid-social-creative, positioning, creative-strategy, partnership-ads, authority
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Fix the attention-retention trade-off across hooks
Use when: Diagnosing an ad with strong 3-second view rate but poor watch-through and poor landing-page conversion.
- Separate 'did we get attention' from 'did we get the right attention'. A confusing hook (a hammer hitting a table) can score high on attention and poor on relevance.
- Check what the viewer actually knows after 3 seconds. If they cannot identify the product category or problem, confusion is masking relevance.
- If a hook was moved from the mid-ad to the opening for a quick test (a valid technique when you have existing footage), check whether it introduced confusion versus clarity — the roofer ad was worse for it.
- Measure both the 3-second hold and the retention curve after the hook to see whether the hook promised something the body delivers.
- Accept that the retention curve only goes down; the aim is to lose fewer of the right people, not to bend the curve upward.
Caveat: The source's roofer example is an anecdote; its 'data shows it isn't working' is assertion, not a cited metric.
Caveat: Not all confusion hooks fail — mystery can work when the payoff is quick and clearly relevant.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, measurement, creative-strategy, video-ads
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Write scripts that do not depend on the founder
Use when: Deciding whether to run a personal-brand-led creative as a cold-audience paid ad versus keeping it organic.
- Identify the load-bearing element of the ad. In the Bryan Johnson example, the entire credibility rests on viewers trusting his longevity mission, not on evidence in the ad.
- Test whether the ad still works with a different face. The source states directly that if you swapped the avatar, this ad would not work at all.
- Make the objective proof visible. The ad claims the competitor product had toxins at seven times the stated level, but shows no test overlays; adding the actual test results would have fixed the central weakness.
- Assume personal-brand ads are bounded by the creator's organic reach. The source's framing: ads should extend past organic, not multiply it.
- If the ad only converts people who already trust the creator, run it organically rather than paying to reach an audience that was going to see it anyway.
Caveat: This is the presenter's strong opinion; deeply trusted founders can convert cold acquisition efficiently in some niches, and no measurement is offered.
Caveat: Even founder-led brands need objective proof available for cold audiences; put the third-party test results on the landing page, not just in the caption.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, positioning, personal-brand, cold-audience
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Audit the click-through destination for live ads
Use when: Running any new or inherited paid social account and checking where active ads actually send traffic.
- Click every live ad from the placement it runs in and confirm the destination page loads.
- The source clicked the Bryan Johnson ad and found a 404 page — the third-party lab results promised in the ad were unreachable.
- Note that the ad's own promise ('you don't have to believe me, third-party lab results are on the website') creates an expectation the landing page must satisfy.
- Treat mismatches between ad claims and destination content as a direct conversion loss, independent of creative performance.
Caveat: URLs and redirects change without warning; check on a schedule, not once.
Caveat: The presenter's check was manual and anecdotal — build a periodic link-check into account maintenance.

Tags: paid-social-creative, landing-page, measurement, acquisition, quality-assurance
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Use a numbered-reasons structure with wide pillar benefits
Use when: Structuring the body of a video ad for a consumable or routine product where you want a simple repeatable frame.
- Open with a curiosity hook and a promise of a specific answer ('how did I add 23g of protein to my favourite cereal?'), then deliver the answer within seconds.
- Use the numbered-reasons format (also common in organic content) to keep structure and pace.
- Choose pillars that keep the addressable market wide. The supplement ad picks energy, focus and recovery — broad needs — rather than 'helps me at the gym, helps me with running', which would narrow to a single avatar.
- Place objection handling inside a numbered reason, not only at the end; the protein ad makes reason three about taste to counter the assumption that plant protein is unpleasant.
- Push table-stakes features (dairy-free, gluten-free, plant-based) through fast rather than dedicating the ad to them.
Caveat: Broad pillars can feel generic; the pillar still needs a supporting detail to be credible.
Caveat: Numbered formats are heavily used and may blend into feed noise — novelty has to come from the specifics.

Tags: paid-social-creative, script-writing, creative-strategy, video-ads, audience-targeting
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Reposition the product against the buyer's self-image objection
Use when: Selling a product category whose buyers reject it because of an image or identity association (for example, ergonomic chairs and the 'gamer' look).
- Name the objection out loud in the script: 'I've been avoiding ergonomic chairs because I don't want to look like a gamer boy.'
- Then reposition the product against the objection: 'it's giving luxury without losing functionality.'
- Use the repositioning to unlock a wider buyer set — in this case an aesthetic-conscious, non-gamer audience.
- Follow the repositioning with proof of the new frame, such as design and development origin.
- Close with CTA language that matches the repositioned value proposition (the chair ad uses 'you should invest in a comfortable working experience', which presupposes long daily use).
Caveat: Repositioning can alienate the original buyer set; check that the new frame doesn't conflict with existing customers.
Caveat: Identity-based objections vary by market and era; verify the association is currently active for your audience.

Tags: paid-social-creative, positioning, objection-handling, video-ads, messaging-positioning
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Recognise the structural signature of ads that burn money
Use when: Auditing a new batch of ads before launch to filter out weak creative cheaply.
- Check for a hook type. If you cannot name the hook type, it is probably not a hook — the Vitable ad simply states 'these are my vital packs'.
- Check when the product or service first appears. All the weak examples introduce it in the first few seconds.
- Check whether the script problem-agitates. The Vitable ad gives no reason to care until 7 to 10 seconds in, and its value proposition only arrives there.
- Check whether the back half is a talking head with no cuts, overlays or social proof floating over it — the roofer ad's second half has this pattern and reads as salesy.
- Check whether the ad says anything about the buyer's lived experience rather than the product's attributes.
- Fix the opening; the later half is often salvageable if the hook and bridge are rebuilt.
Caveat: These are qualitative patterns from six ads in mixed niches, not a validated scoring system.
Caveat: Some categories (for example, urgency-driven commodity offers) can tolerate product-first hooks; verify against your own performance data.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, ad-review, video-ads, conversion-optimization
Source: Why These 3 Ads Crushed It (And Why The Others Burned Money).docx

---

## Deconstruct Any Ad Into Messaging and Creative DNA
Use when: Auditing a competitor's winning ad or your own top performer to decide what to iterate on next for your store's creative pipeline.
- Treat every ad as two layers: messaging (what you say) and creative (what it looks like — video or image).
- Split messaging into three questions: WHO = the segment; WHAT = concept, angle, mechanism, copy blocks; HOW = hook and format.
- For video, add the CAST breakdown: Conceit (media format the ad pretends to be, e.g. interview), Actor, Style (visual gloss — realistic, Pixar, claymation), Terrain (environment), Setup (captions, B-roll, inserts, split screen, hook stacking).
- Worked example from the source: an anti-aging ad had segment = broad anti-aging; concept = men cheat because their wife stops glowing; angle = confessional whistleblower; mechanism = low-molecular-weight collagen and hyaluronic acid absorbing into skin; format = confessional/interview-style; conceit = pretends to be an interview; actor = high-end escort; style = realistic; terrain = dark studio; setup = captions plus testimonial/demo splices.
- When an ad wins, ask which element is responsible — the concept alone, the actor alone, or the combination — and rotate the others.
- Apply this to your store by writing the DNA breakdown for your top 5 and bottom 5 ads, then hypothesizing what to keep and what to swap.
Caveat: The grammar describes most of an ad but not all of it; other components exist.
Caveat: This is an analytical framework, not proof any single element causes performance.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, ad-analysis, video-ads, competitor-research, iteration
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Build Audience Segments From Outcome × Demographic × Facet
Use when: Expanding creative targeting for a supplements, health, skincare or similar store by finding untapped customer slices to write dedicated ads for.
- Treat a segment as a slice of a market defined on three dimensions: outcome (core thing they want or don't want), demographic (gender, race, age, buyer type), and facet (a specific condition, state or event).
- List outcomes for your product; for a testosterone product the source gives examples like better sex, losing man boobs, losing weight.
- List facets such as smoker, on finasteride, considering TRT, vegetarian/vegan, GLP-1 user, diabetic, runner.
- Multiply dimensions as few or as many as you want to get segments like 'all men who want better sex', 'all black men struggling with erections', or 'men over 50 on finasteride who want to lose weight'.
- Run these as separate ad concepts so the algorithm serves each segment independently.
- Call this horizontal scaling, and prioritize new outcomes and sub-outcomes plus multiplying facets as the highest-gain area.
Caveat: Segment definitions must respect ad platform targeting and health-claim policies; verify current rules.
Caveat: Granularity is a spectrum — the source says you can go broad or narrow, not that one is universally better.

Tags: paid-social-creative, audience-segmentation, customer-research, acquisition, creative-strategy, horizontal-scaling
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Separate Concept, Angle and Mechanism Before Writing Copy
Use when: Planning the messaging layer of a new ad or landing page angle for a product whose core claim is still vague.
- Write the concept as one plain sentence — the core idea you're communicating. Source example: too much vitamin D is bad for you.
- Choose an angle: the same concept can be shown as a conspiracy about the vitamin D industry, a whistleblower, or a side-by-side comparison of two people with and without vitamin D.
- Define the mechanism in two parts — the unique mechanism of the problem (why do I have this problem?) and the unique mechanism of the solution (how is your product perfect for it?).
- Prefer mechanisms that are simple, intuitive and new; typically one problem and one solution.
- Strengthen the mechanism by tying in founder story, product creation story, unique features/ingredients, unique characterizations and moments in time to deepen positioning.
- Only after concept, angle and mechanism are clear, write the hook from that idea so messaging stays coherent.
Caveat: The source allows multi-problem/multi-solution mechanisms but flags the one-problem/one-solution form as the typical best pattern.
Caveat: Mechanism claims must be substantiated to avoid regulatory issues in health and supplement categories.

Tags: paid-social-creative, messaging, positioning, offer-design, creative-strategy, product-page
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Use the Six Copy Blocks as a Persuasion Checklist
Use when: Reviewing ad scripts or product page copy before launch to find which persuasion blocks are missing or weak.
- List the six copy blocks: pain, promise, proof, curiosity, constraints, conditions.
- Score each ad's strength on each block — in the source's dissection, one ad had pain, promise and curiosity present but weak, and other blocks missing; a second ad had all blocks present but proof was weak.
- Treat it as a checklist: more blocks present and stronger usually means a better ad.
- Where proof is weak, strengthen it with testimonials, demo splices or ingredient data rather than adding new claims.
- Use the audit output to decide the single block to fix on the next iteration instead of rewriting the whole ad.
Caveat: The source calls this a typical pattern, not a guaranteed causal rule; having all six does not ensure an ad wins.
Caveat: Constraint and condition blocks are context-specific and may not fit every offer.

Tags: paid-social-creative, copywriting, conversion-optimization, product-page, creative-strategy, ad-analysis
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Write Hooks in Four Steps: Isolate, Commit, Deep Fry, Bang
Use when: Drafting the first 3 seconds of a video ad or the headline of a static ad for a new product angle.
- Isolate the idea: start from the core product mechanism — e.g. a magnesium cream that's better than capsules for pain.
- Commit to the idea fully before embellishing it.
- Deep fry it: add contrast, surprise, novelty, nuance and stakes. Ask 'why is it better?' — it absorbs through skin — then stretch the feature into something interesting: a specific magnesium with no attached molecule becomes 'naked magnesium'.
- Make it bang: add emotional trigger words, keep it simple, prioritize the best information, and add open loops. Example result: a grandma used a weird naked magnesium trick that fixed her back pain in two weeks — contrast between grandma and naked, emotional charge on weird/naked, and an open loop from 'trick'.
- Treat hooks as roughly 90% of the messaging job and iterate multiple hooks per single ad idea.
Caveat: The source explicitly says the example hook is illustrative, not proven to work — do not treat it as a template that guarantees results.
Caveat: Emotional and 'naked'-style framing must fit brand and platform policy.

Tags: paid-social-creative, hook-writing, video-ads, copywriting, creative-strategy, acquisition
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Pretend-Format Ads: Pick a Conceit That Fits the Offer
Use when: Deciding the structural format for a new ad, or refreshing a winning concept that is fatiguing.
- Understand format as the structure and way you approach the idea; formats trend and rotate over time.
- Recognize the recurring formats the source names: whistleblower, timeline ads, personification (e.g. animated body parts), and Wall Street Journal style (comparing two people's paths side by side).
- Note that not every ad needs a conceit — when nothing is being imitated, skip that CAST element (the personified-arteries ad had no conceit).
- When you have a winning concept or angle, apply it to a known winning format and winning style to shortcut production.
- When iterating a winner, change the format first: keep concept and actor, then test the same ad as a podcast, an on-stage talk, a street interview or a slice-of-life film.
Caveat: Formats are trend-dependent — what is winning now may not be next quarter, so monitor current accounts.
Caveat: Pretending to be a real media format may raise disclosure or authenticity concerns depending on platform policy.

Tags: paid-social-creative, ad-formats, video-ads, creative-strategy, iteration, concept-testing

## Three Creative Types Across Three Funnel Stages
Use when: Auditing your ad account's creative mix to see whether every funnel stage has the right creative type for your store.
- Sort every creative into one of three types: branded, template (follows a pre-assigned template structure), and native (looks like realistic, non-ad content).
- Map creatives against funnel position: bottom, middle and top of funnel.
- For top of funnel, favor native and trend-driven styles that blend into the feed; for bottom of funnel, favor clearer branded or direct-response formats.
- Use the matrix to spot empty cells — e.g. no native top-of-funnel tests or no branded bottom-of-funnel retargeting — and fill those gaps in the next production cycle.
- Re-evaluate the classification regularly as formats evolve.
Caveat: The source offers this as a mental model without detailed examples; exact assignments depend on your market and platform.
Caveat: Funnel-stage definitions vary by advertiser; align with your own account structure rather than an absolute rule.

Tags: paid-social-creative, funnel-strategy, creative-strategy, ad-formats, acquisition, retention
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

## Use the CAST System to Clone Winning Videos Element by Element
Use when: Producing multiple video ad variants from a single proven winner for a store without losing what made it work.
- Decompose the video: Conceit = the media format it pretends to be; Actor = who appears (including personified objects like arteries or product); Style = visual gloss (realistic, Pixar, claymation, felt); Terrain = environment and setting; Setup = chunky production elements like captions, B-roll, inserts, cutaways, split screen, hook stacking.
- Verify against the source's example: the personified-arteries ad had no conceit, actors were the arteries/clogs/product/ingredients, style was Pixar-like, terrain was inside the body, setup was captions only.
- Hypothesize which element drives the win — the concept, the actor, or the combination.
- If the actor drives it (e.g. the escort-confessional ad), make more ads with that actor type and rotate angle, mechanism, hook and format.
- If the concept drives it, keep the concept and swap actor, style or terrain.
- Produce iterations by selling a winning reference to your editor with the specific element changed, rather than briefing from scratch.
Caveat: The framework approximates the ad but does not fully describe it; use judgment on which elements matter.
Caveat: Role-based scripting or actors may face platform impersonation or disclosure policies.

Tags: paid-social-creative, video-ads, iteration, creative-strategy, production, ad-analysis
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

## Pattern-Detect Winners and Losers With Shared Grammar
Use when: Reviewing account and competitor performance data to decide which creative elements to double down on for your store.
- Tag every ad you see — your own, competitor, and organic content — using the same element labels (segment, concept, angle, mechanism, copy blocks, hook, format; conceit, actor, style, terrain, setup).
- Ask of each ad: why is this working, kind of working, or not working?
- Compare across sources: your account, competitor accounts, and the outside world (organic content, forums, comments, reviews).
- Form explicit hypotheses from the detected patterns and test them as new ad variations.
- Use consistent labels so that AI tools can process the tagged data and detect correlations you'd miss manually.
- Feed findings back into rotation decisions — which element to change, which to hold.
Caveat: Pattern detection produces hypotheses, not proof; correlation in your own data may not transfer to competitors or other markets.
Caveat: AI-assisted tagging quality depends on your labeling consistency and model; verify outputs.

Tags: paid-social-creative, measurement, creative-strategy, ad-analysis, competitor-research, experiment-design
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Find Account Gaps With the Segment-Format Matrix
Use when: Planning the next month's creative testing calendar for a store and tired of producing variations on the same theme.
- Build a matrix of your possible segments against possible formats and styles.
- Mark which combinations you've already run and which competitors are running.
- Flag gaps: if competitors are heavy on timeline ads and you have none, that's a gap; if they use a new style you don't, that's another gap.
- Also mine segments buried in comments and Amazon reviews as untapped targeting opportunities.
- Prioritize the gaps for the next test batch, mixing in proven cells so the account keeps working while you test net-new areas.
- Repeat the matrix review regularly as formats and competitor behavior shift.
Caveat: The source gives no numeric target for how many gap tests to run; it depends on production capacity and spend.
Caveat: Testing net-new formats typically has a lower hit rate than iterating winners.

Tags: paid-social-creative, competitor-research, creative-strategy, experiment-design, customer-research, planning
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## STORMING: Diversify Idea Sources So You Never Run Out
Use when: Filling a creative pipeline for frequent ad testing so ideas are not all rip-offs of the same competitor or your own brain.
- Swipes: pull winning ad ideas from competitor accounts.
- Templates: reuse proven formats and structures.
- Organic research: mine comments, Amazon reviews, Facebook comments, forums and new papers for language and angles.
- Matrix: cross segments with formats to reveal missing combinations.
- Internal vectors: identify patterns inside your own account data.
- New styles: adopt trending visual styles such as Pixar or claymation looks.
- Gambit: allow shower-thought wild cards — low hit rate but occasionally bangers when executed well.
- Track which source each test came from so you can see whether your diversification is real.
Caveat: Gambits are explicitly low-probability; don't let them crowd out higher-likelihood work.
Caveat: Sourcing organic content as idea input doesn't license copying it directly.

Tags: paid-social-creative, idea-generation, creative-strategy, research, planning, experiment-design
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

---

## Make Strategic Bets: Cheap Iterations Usually Beat Net-New Ads
Use when: Deciding what to produce this week when editor capacity and testing budget are the real constraints for your store.
- Recognize the real bottleneck: at 10 ads/week you have few shots; at 50 it matters less; editor capacity and how many ads you can actually test are hard limits even with AI.
- Answer explicit strategy questions per batch: iterate a winner or make something net new? Which sources to pull from? Which ideas to prioritize? More proven or more wild card? New segments or deepen existing ones? Focus on new styles, formats or conceits?
- Lean toward the source's stated high-leverage defaults: new conceits/formats on winning ideas, new facets and sub-outcomes on winning ideas, new styles on winning ideas, and applying winning styles across past winning ideas.
- Keep it simple — the source says the easiest answer is normally the right one and that most people abandon winning ideas earlier than the market does.
- Scale up net-new bets only when capacity makes the trade-off cheap.
Caveat: The source explicitly says there is no one-size-fits-all answer; these are common-sense defaults, not universal rules.
Caveat: Bottleneck numbers (10 vs 50 ads/week) are illustrative examples, not benchmarks.

Tags: paid-social-creative, experiment-design, creative-strategy, planning, iteration, budgeting
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

## Follow a Simple Production Workflow From Idea to Editor
Use when: Running the day-to-day production process for paid social assets without overcomplicating the handoff.
- Pick the idea from your prioritized test list.
- Write one or more hooks for that idea before anything else.
- Draft the rest of the ad with AI, doing light manual editing on the major points — especially the mechanism and copy blocks.
- The source's view: if the hook is right and the AI writing process is good, the rest of the ad often writes itself.
- Produce static images separately, then send video work to an editor paired with a winning reference ad.
- Verify the current capabilities of whatever AI production tool you use, since interfaces change.
Caveat: Source describes tooling from a specific platform it promotes; the workflow principle transfers, the tooling claims are not independently verified.
Caveat: AI drafts still need human review for claims, compliance and brand voice.

Tags: paid-social-creative, production, video-ads, workflow, hook-writing, static-ads
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

## Run a Weekly Refinement Loop and Accept Most Ads Fail
Use when: Setting the operating rhythm for a store's paid social creative program so decisions get updated instead of set once.
- Schedule account and competitor reviews at least a few times per week, not just at monthly reporting.
- At each review, examine which new ads are winning or losing and why, using the ad DNA and CAST vocabulary.
- Update your priorities and focus based on both the data and your developing judgment.
- Expect most ads to fail and treat that as normal, not a signal to stop; the job is to keep finding the few that work.
- Protect morale and pace by keeping the process mechanical and letting it stay enjoyable, since the source argues enjoyment produces better outcomes.
Caveat: The source does not give a specific win-rate percentage, only that most ads fail; don't invent a benchmark.
Caveat: The link between enjoying the work and outcome quality is the presenter's opinion, not a tested finding.

Tags: paid-social-creative, measurement, creative-strategy, workflow, experiment-design, retention
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

## Develop Taste by Deliberately Practicing Ad Diagnosis
Use when: Leveling up your own or a team member's creative judgment so faster, better test-prioritization decisions get made for the store.
- Practice decomposing many ads — winners and losers — into the DNA and CAST elements on a regular basis.
- After each observation, write a hypothesis about why the ad performs as it does.
- Watch for subtle, hard-to-articulate patterns across many ads; the source compares this to expert intuition documented in examples like chicken sexers in the book Blink.
- Use those accumulated instincts specifically at the prioritization stage, where the source says taste matters most.
- Keep the practice mechanical and explicit rather than waiting for intuition to arrive on its own.
Caveat: Intuition can encode bias from limited exposure; keep testing hypotheses rather than trusting taste blindly.
Caveat: The chicken-sexer and Blink references are analogies, not evidence about advertising outcomes.

Tags: paid-social-creative, creative-strategy, ad-analysis, experiment-design, skill-development, measurement

## Reduce Video Ads to Element Rotation for Efficient Iteration
Use when: Scaling a proven video ad into a batch of variants for a store without wasting editor time on unfocused changes.
- Start from a confirmed winner and list its CAST plus messaging elements.
- Form a hypothesis about the active ingredient — the concept, the actor, or their combination.
- If the actor is the driver, produce more ads with a comparable actor and swap angle, mechanism, hook and format.
- If the concept is the driver, keep the concept and change actor, style, terrain or setup.
- If concept and actor both matter in combination, hold both and vary angle, mechanism, hook, or the conceit (e.g. podcast, on-stage talk, street interview, slice-of-life film).
- Label each variant by the single changed element so results attribute cleanly.
Caveat: Changing only one element per test is cleaner for attribution but slower; the source permits bolder multi-element bets as situational alternatives.
Caveat: Platform algorithms may serve variants against each other unpredictably; interpret results with caution.

Tags: paid-social-creative, iteration, video-ads, experiment-design, creative-strategy, measurement
Source: creative strategy is easy actually.docx

## Diagnose an Ad by Mapping It to Segment, Concept, Angle and Mechanism
Use when: Reviewing a draft ad or existing top performer for a store to see if the underlying idea is strong enough before scaling spend.
- Write one line each for: segment (who), concept (core idea), angle (how it's presented), mechanism (problem cause plus solution cause), copy blocks present, hook, and format.
- Compare against the source's diabetic men with ED example: segment = diabetic men with ED; concept = high blood sugar scars arteries, harming erections; angle = personified object; mechanism = five science-backed ingredients; all copy blocks present but proof weak; format = personified body part.
- Check coherence: does the mechanism connect the problem cause to the solution cause in a simple, intuitive, new way?
- Check completeness: which copy blocks are missing or weak and is the hook isolated, committed, deep-fried and loaded with open loops?
- Use the diagnosis to fix the weakest layer first rather than reshooting the whole ad.
Caveat: These are example ads from the presenter's accounts; replicating a mechanism does not promise the same result in another market.
Caveat: Health-related mechanisms require substantiation and compliant claims.

Tags: paid-social-creative, ad-analysis, messaging, creative-strategy, video-ads, product-page
